**Trap:** A rumour causing disruption is not the same as an organised operation; attribution is not proven.
**Mains hook:** Use as an example of essential-service disruption; qualify.
**Back:** *"Twenty-Twenty rumour, stations shut for a day, not a war."*

### Flashcard IS-296 — Zhenhua Data case (state-linked data harvesting)
**Front:** What does the Zhenhua Data case illustrate?
**Definition (learn):** A state-linked entity built relational databases recording associations between individuals, institutions and information across social media, papers, patents and bidding documents to offer "threat intelligence services" (VisionIAS PDF p. 19).
**Breakdown:**
- Type: state-capacity-scale data harvesting.
- Scope: cross-platform, global, including India (adv §7).
- Difference from individual fake-news posts: scale and intent.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 19 (✅ owner 09 adv §2, §7). ⚠️ Company name and date are owner-given; not fetched.
**Links:** IS-280, IS-297, IS-312.
**Trap:** Do not conflate a state-linked data-harvesting case with a domestic fake-news incident; they need different responses (adv §3).
**Mains hook:** Use to separate state-linked hybrid threats from non-state amplification.
**Back:** *"Zhenhua maps the people, not the posts."*

### Flashcard IS-297 — Extraterritorial enforcement limit
**Front:** Why can domestic law not fully address foreign-based operations?
**Definition (learn):** VisionIAS (PDF p. 19): "enforcement of privacy laws in a foreign jurisdiction is almost impossible because they differ from one country to another." Domestic law cannot reach foreign-based operations alone.
**Breakdown:**
- Limit: jurisdictional differences.
- Response: international cooperation (Christchurch Call-style).
- Domestic law = necessary, not sufficient.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 19 (✅ owner 09 adv §2, §6).
**Links:** IS-291, IS-296, IS-299.
**Trap:** International cooperation is a supplement, not a substitute for domestic law (adv §8).
**Mains hook:** Use to show the need for diplomatic and intelligence channels.
**Back:** *"Foreign servers, Indian law: cooperate or concede."*

### Flashcard IS-298 — National Counter Ransomware Task Force (MHA)
**Front:** What is the National Counter Ransomware Task Force and where does its overlap lie?
**Definition (learn):** An MHA-approved body cited by VisionIAS (PDF p. 27) primarily for cybercrime coordination, with overlap into information warfare where ransomware is used as a coercion tool.
**Breakdown:**
- Parent: MHA-approved (owner).
- Remit: cybercrime coordination.
- Overlap: coercion via ransomware in information operations.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 27 (✅ owner 09 basic §4). ⚠️ Constitution year and composition not verified.
**Links:** IS-250, IS-256.
**Trap:** Do not name a specific office holder or year without a dated source.
**Mains hook:** Use under investigation layer; qualify.
**Back:** *"Ransom task force: crime desk with a coercion overlap."*

### Flashcard IS-299 — Fact-check unit amendment (2023) and Bombay High Court (2024) — ⚠️ conflicting status
**Front:** What did the 2023 fact-check unit amendment do and what happened in court?
**Definition (learn):** A 2023 amendment to the 2021 Rules empowered a government fact-check unit to flag online content about the Union Government's business as false. Owner 09 states the Bombay High Court struck the provision down in 2024 as violative of Articles 14 and 19(1)(a).
**Breakdown:**
- Subject: content about Union Government business.
- Owner: struck down 2024 (Articles 14, 19(1)(a)).
- ⚠️ Conflict: the Wikipedia fetch on the 2021 Rules did not mention the amendment; my recollection is that the 2024 Bombay HC decision on this challenge was a split verdict, not a clean strike-down. Verify the judgment and MeitY notification before quoting "struck down."
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2023 amendment; Bombay HC 2024 (📰 owner 09 basic §4; ⚠️ not verified by fetch).
**Links:** IS-282, IS-300, IS-301, IS-302.
**Trap:** "Struck down" is claimed by the owner; do not state it as settled without the judgment (owner itself marks ⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Use as an illustration that the state cannot be the arbiter of truth about itself; qualify with the pending or split status.
**Back:** *"Fact-check flagged the government's facts; the court is still deciding how."*

### Flashcard IS-300 — Grievance Appellate Committee (GAC), Rule 3A
**Front:** What is the GAC, what does it decide, and how is it different from a s.69A block?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 09 §14 states that the 2022 amendment introduced the GAC under Rule 3A as a government-run appeal forum against a platform Grievance Officer's decision; three GACs were notified in 2023 as three-member online appellate bodies with a 30-day disposal target and binding orders on intermediaries.
**Breakdown:**
- Function: appeals from users against platform decisions.
- Orders: binding on the intermediary; may direct removal or restoration.
- Target: 30 days (owner).
- Online portal: gac.gov.in (owner).
- Not the s.69A government blocking route.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Rule 3A; 2022 amendment; 2023 GAC notification (📰 owner 09 §14; ⚠️ not verified by fetch).
**Links:** IS-283, IS-287, IS-299.
**Trap:** GAC ≠ Fact-Check Unit (IS-299). Membership changes; do not memorise a chair. ⚠️ Composition conflict: REVISION-CHART §14 says the GAC is "chaired by I4C's CEO"; owner 09 §14 says government-appointed three-member committees. Verify the notified composition before answering.
**Mains hook:** Use as an appeal layer; qualify with independence and government-appointed composition.
**Back:** *"Rule Three-A: an appeal court for users, not a censor for the state."*

### Flashcard IS-301 — Feb 2026 synthetic-information (deepfake) amendment, G.S.R. 120(E) — ⚠️ unverified
**Front:** What does the 10 February 2026 amendment introduce?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 09 §14 states a 10 February 2026 amendment (G.S.R. 120(E)) introduces "Synthetically Generated Information" (AI/deepfake) with labelling and disclosure obligations and shortens compliance to 3 hours for specified government/court removal orders.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 10 February 2026 (owner).
- Definition: Synthetically Generated Information.
- Obligation: labelling/disclosure.
- Window: 3 hours for specified removal orders.
- Sensitive impersonation: separate expedited route (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** G.S.R. 120(E), 10 Feb 2026 (📰 owner 09 §14; ⚠️ NOT fetched; verify the gazette text before quoting any clause).
**Links:** IS-282, IS-299, IS-300.
**Trap:** The 2026 amendment is one link in a chronology, not the origin of the Rules (owner trap). Do not quote clause numbers without the gazette. ⚠️ Date conflict: the REVISION-CHART (§14) gives "6 Feb 2026" for the same amendment; owner 09 gives 10 February 2026 (G.S.R. 120(E)). Verify the gazette date before quoting.
**Mains hook:** Use under "amendments and salient features"; qualify that the exact wording requires gazette verification.
**Back:** *"Three hours for the fake, labels for the synthetic."*

### Flashcard IS-302 — Sahyog portal (government-intermediary takedown routing) — ⚠️ unverified
**Front:** What is the Sahyog portal and what criticism does the owner record?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 09 §14 describes Sahyog as an automated portal for routing government blocking and takedown notices to intermediaries, criticised for potentially bypassing the Section 69A procedural safeguards (written order, review committee) that Shreya Singhal required.
**Breakdown:**
- Function: automated routing of takedown notices.
- Criticism: bypass of 69A safeguards (owner).
- Status: live civil-liberties objection, not a settled legal position (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Sahyog portal (📰 owner 09 §14; ⚠️ Wikipedia fetch returned 404; not verified).
**Links:** IS-287, IS-290, IS-299.
**Trap:** Do not state the portal's legal basis or launch year; not verified here.
**Mains hook:** Use as the live example of the platform-state boundary; qualify with the unresolved status.
**Back:** *"Sahyog routes the notice; Shreya asked who signs."*

### Flashcard IS-303 — 2012 Facebook-comment arrest and Aseem Trivedi sedition case (book examples)
**Front:** What two 2012 cases does Singh cite for the free-expression/security-law tension?
**Definition (learn):** Singh (PDF p. 118) cites the 2012 arrest of two women over a Facebook comment (later withdrawn) sparking debate on Section 66A misuse, and cartoonist Aseem Trivedi's 2012 sedition arrest over anti-corruption cartoons.
**Breakdown:**
- Case 1: 2012 Facebook comment arrest (66A debate).
- Case 2: Aseem Trivedi, 2012 sedition arrest.
- Status: superseded in legal terms by Shreya Singhal (2015) for 66A.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 118 (✅ owner 09 basic §5).
**Links:** IS-263, IS-290, IS-278.
**Trap:** These are book-period examples; do not cite them as current law.
**Mains hook:** Use as historical illustration of rights tension; qualify with later holdings.
**Back:** *"Twenty-Twelve: a comment and a cartoon tested the law."*

### Flashcard IS-304 — Encryption's dual character (protection vs coordination)
**Front:** Why is "ban encryption" not a valid security remedy?
**Definition (learn):** Encryption protects legitimate commerce, journalism and personal privacy while also enabling hard-to-monitor terrorist or criminal coordination. The security problem lies in misuse, not in encryption's existence; a blanket weakening degrades security for all users.
**Breakdown:**
- Legitimate uses: commerce, journalism, personal privacy.
- Misuse: coordination, radicalisation.
- Calibrated remedy: lawful access with judicial oversight, narrowly scoped.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 09 adv §2, §6 (⚠️ analytical inference).
**Links:** IS-285, IS-305, IS-310.
**Trap:** "Encryption is illegal" or "ban it" is a close-option trap; examiners reward noting legitimate use.
**Mains hook:** "Critically examine lawful-access mechanisms" (15 marks); qualify with architectural limits.
**Back:** *"Protect the lock, police the misuse."*

### Flashcard IS-305 — Metadata traceability vs content access (the fault line)
**Front:** Why is Rule 4(2) a metadata question and not a decryption question?
**Definition (learn):** The government's answer is that Rule 4(2) demands the first originator of a specific message, a metadata identification, not the content. Platforms reply that attributing origination at scale in an end-to-end encrypted system needs a protocol change (system-wide, e.g. provenance retention for all users).
**Breakdown:**
- Legal demand: identify first originator on a specified order.
- Platform objection: architectural change for all users.
- Status: in litigation (owner); the dispute is architectural, not evidentiary.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 09 adv §2 (⚠️ analytical framing; WhatsApp suit 25 May 2021 per Wikipedia).
**Links:** IS-285, IS-304, IS-310.
**Trap:** The rule does not demand message content; the platform objection is about architecture, not the rule's text.
**Mains hook:** State the rule, state the objection, do not declare a winner; qualify with proportionality.
**Back:** *"First hand is metadata; the whole message is the fight."*

### Flashcard IS-306 — Puttaswamy (2017) four-part proportionality test
**Front:** What standard does K.S. Puttaswamy (2017) provide for content restriction and surveillance?
**Definition (learn):** The Supreme Court's 2017 privacy judgment laid down a four-part proportionality test relevant to content restriction and surveillance measures (owner 09 §14).
**Breakdown:**
- Test: proportionality (four parts per owner; enumerated parts not reproduced in owner text).
- Application: any content-restriction or surveillance measure.
- Use: anchor for a rights standard alongside Anuradha Bhasin.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** K.S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India (2017) (📰 owner 09 §14; ⚠️ the four prongs are not enumerated in the owner text; not fetched).
**Links:** IS-289, IS-290, IS-288.
**Trap:** Do not list the four prongs from memory in an exam without the judgment text.
**Mains hook:** Use as the general privacy/proportionality standard; qualify.
**Back:** *"Twenty-Seventeen: privacy first, then proportion."*

### Flashcard IS-307 — Over-removal incentive under safe-harbour enforcement
**Front:** Why does safe-harbour enforcement create over-removal?
**Definition (learn):** Because Rule 7's sanction is loss of s.79 immunity rather than a content-specific penalty, an intermediary's rational response to ambiguity is to remove more, not less (owner 09 adv §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: liability for third-party content.
- Response: remove more under ambiguity.
- Remedy: transparency reporting on takedown volumes.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 09 adv §4, §6 (⚠️ analytical inference).
**Links:** IS-286, IS-283, IS-317.
**Trap:** Good-faith drafting does not remove the structural incentive (owner adv §6).
**Mains hook:** Use under platform-accountability critique; qualify with transparency.
**Back:** *"Fear of liability removes more than the rumour."*

### Flashcard IS-308 — Microsoft 2020 survey: 64% encountered fake news (survey figure)
**Front:** What survey figure does VisionIAS cite on fake news exposure?
**Definition (learn):** A 2020 Microsoft study cited by VisionIAS found 64% of surveyed Indians had encountered fake news online.
**Breakdown:**
- Year: 2020.
- Source: Microsoft study (survey, not government).
- Figure: 64% (cited).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 09 basic §6 (⚠️ survey, not official; not fetched).
**Links:** IS-281, IS-279.
**Trap:** A private survey figure is not an official statistic; label it so.
**Mains hook:** Use only as illustrative context; qualify.
**Back:** *"Sixty-four per cent saw it, one survey said."*

### Flashcard IS-309 — Singapore POFMA (2019) as a comparator
**Front:** Which foreign comparator does VisionIAS use for legal framework strengthening against fake news?
**Definition (learn):** VisionIAS (PDF p. 28) cites Singapore's 2019 Protection from Online Falsehood and Manipulation Act (POFMA) as an analogue for legal-framework strengthening.
**Breakdown:**
- Country: Singapore.
- Year: 2019.
- Use: comparator for legal framework (owner adv §4).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** POFMA 2019 (✅ owner 09 adv §4). ⚠️ Act name and year from owner; not fetched.
**Links:** IS-282, IS-299.
**Trap:** A foreign comparator is not an Indian law; do not transplant the Act without constitutional analysis.
**Mains hook:** Use as comparative example; qualify with free-speech objection.
**Back:** *"Singapore's falsehood act: a comparator, not a template."*

### Flashcard IS-310 — PYQ 2024 Q20: social media and encrypted messaging measures (15 marks)
**Front:** What does the 2024 PYQ demand and how should the answer be structured?
**Definition (learn):** "Social media and encrypting messaging services pose a serious security challenge. What measures have been adopted at various levels to address the security implications of social media? Also suggest any other remedies to address the problem." (Suggest measures · 15 marks · 250 words.)
**Breakdown:**
- Start: four implications (radicalisation/coordination, disinformation, identity-conflict amplification, foreign/hybrid influence).
- Six-level response: content (69A, Rule 3(1)(d)); platform (Rules 3, 4(1), 7); encrypted messaging (Rule 4(2)); network (Telecom Act 2023 + Anuradha Bhasin); investigation/community (I4C, State police, media literacy); external (Christchurch, CTC Delhi Declaration).
- Additional remedies: media literacy, verified channels, early warning, transparency reporting.
- Reject encryption ban and default shutdown.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2024 GS-III Q20 (✅ owner 09 basic §9, §12; routed ledger `_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-GS3-GS4-2024-2025.md`).
**Links:** IS-282-IS-291, IS-304, IS-305, IS-313.
**Trap:** Do not turn the content answer into a blanket encryption ban; do not confuse incident and cybercrime.
**Mains hook:** Six-level spine + reasoned additional remedies; qualify with rights safeguards.
**Back:** *"Six levels named, encryption kept, shutdown refused."*

### Flashcard IS-311 — PYQ 2026 GS-III Q9: fake news threat and IT Rules amendments (10 marks)
**Front:** What does the 2026 GS-III Q9 ask and what is the answer spine?
**Definition (learn):** "Explain how fake news and disinformation pose threat to Internal Security and Public Order in Indian context? In this regard, discuss salient features of amendments in respect of Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules 2021." (10 marks, 150 words.)
**Breakdown:**
- Threat (3-4 lines): communal violence, panic displacement, disrupted services, hybrid tool; cite Muzaffarnagar 2013 or 2020 Delhi rumour.
- Amendments (4-5 lines): 2021 base → 2022/2023 GAC (Rule 3A) → 2023 FCU amendment (struck down per owner) → 10 Feb 2026 SGI amendment with 3-hour window.
- Qualify (1-2 lines): Sahyog bypass criticism; verify SGI clause wording.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2026 GS-III Q9 (📰 owner 09 §14; OCR of local paper; ⚠️ Chronology items IS-299-IS-302 unverified).
**Links:** IS-293, IS-295, IS-282, IS-299-IS-302.
**Trap:** The 2026 amendment is one step, not the origin of the framework.
**Mains hook:** Chronological spine with qualified conclusion (trusted communication is a preventive capability).
**Back:** *"Threat first, amendments in order, then qualify the portal."*

### Flashcard IS-312 — Muzaffarnagar and Zhenhua as boundary cases (IR vs Internal Security)
**Front:** Why are the Zhenhua case and the identity-conflict cases boundary cases?
**Definition (learn):** The Zhenhua case straddles IR (diplomatic dimension) and Internal Security (domestic legal gap). The 2012/2013 cases straddle communication-network threat and communal violence, which is why this folder treats identity-conflict amplification inside the topic.
**Breakdown:**
- Zhenhua: IR owns diplomacy; IS owns what domestic law can reach.
- 2012/2013 cases: amplification mechanism is the common thread.
- Topics 02, 04, 05 are cross-referenced.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 09 adv §5 (⚠️ analytical boundary).
**Links:** IS-293, IS-294, IS-296.
**Trap:** Do not present identity-conflict as a standalone communalism topic in this folder (owner 09 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Use to show boundary routing; qualify.
**Back:** *"Diplomacy belongs to IR, domestic reach to us."*

### Flashcard IS-313 — Multi-level response architecture (six levels)
**Front:** Name the six response levels for social-media security.
**Definition (learn):** Content (s.69A, Rule 3(1)(d)); platform (Rules 3, 4(1), 7); encrypted messaging (Rule 4(2)); network (Telecom Act 2023); investigation/community (I4C, State police, media literacy); external (Christchurch Call, CTC Delhi Declaration).
**Breakdown:**
- Level 1: content.
- Level 2: platform.
- Level 3: encrypted messaging.
- Level 4: network.
- Level 5: investigation and community.
- Level 6: external cooperation.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 09 basic §3.5, §12 (✅ owner).
**Links:** IS-287, IS-283, IS-285, IS-288, IS-250, IS-291.
**Trap:** The levels are not interchangeable; a single layer (e.g. takedown) cannot address both state-linked and non-state threats (adv §3).
**Mains hook:** Use as the answer skeleton; end with a verdict on additional remedies.
**Back:** *"Content, Platform, Message, Network, Community, World: six doors."*

### Flashcard IS-314 — Shutdown as last resort, not default
**Front:** Why is an internet shutdown not a proportionate default remedy?
**Definition (learn):** Shutdowns carry economic and rights costs, do not address the underlying disinformation dynamic, and are at best a short-term, exceptional containment tool. Anuradha Bhasin requires orders to be published, reasoned, necessary, proportionate, time-bound and reviewed.
**Breakdown:**
- Costs: economic, rights.
- Limits: do not address dynamics.
- Legal standard: Anuradha Bhasin (IS-289).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 09 basic §7, adv §6 (⚠️ analytical; Anuradha Bhasin per owner).
**Links:** IS-289, IS-288, IS-315.
**Trap:** Shutdown is not a default answer.
**Mains hook:** "Proportionality, not prohibition" 15-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Shutdown is a bandage, not the surgery."*

### Flashcard IS-315 — Anuradha Bhasin's reviewability trap
**Front:** Is an internet shutdown an unreviewable executive prerogative?
**Definition (learn):** No. Anuradha Bhasin (2020) made suspension orders publishable, reviewable and subject to a proportionality and periodic-review test (owner 09 adv §8).
**Breakdown:**
- Publication: required.
- Review: judicial, periodic.
- Proportionality: required.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Anuradha Bhasin (2020) (✅ owner 09 adv §8).
**Links:** IS-289, IS-314.
**Trap:** Executive prerogative is the wrong frame; the Court's review is the standard.
**Mains hook:** Use to qualify any argument for unreviewed power.
**Back:** *"No prerogative, only published, reviewed orders."*

### Flashcard IS-316 — Rule 4(2) vs breaking encryption (trap)
**Front:** Why are Rule 4(2) traceability and breaking encryption not the same demand?
**Definition (learn):** Rule 4(2) requires identification of the first originator of a specific message on an order, a metadata attribution. Breaking encryption would require decrypting content. The platform objection is architectural.
**Breakdown:**
- Rule 4(2): first originator, metadata.
- Breaking encryption: content decryption.
- Platform objection: architectural change at scale.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 09 basic §7 (✅ owner).
**Links:** IS-285, IS-305.
**Trap:** Do not say the rule requires message content.
**Mains hook:** State the rule and objection; do not decide the dispute.
**Back:** *"Find the sender, do not read the letter."*

### Flashcard IS-317 — Transparency reporting as a cheap remedy
**Front:** Why does the owner call transparency reporting on takedowns a cheap and genuine remedy?
**Definition (learn):** Transparency reporting on takedown and shutdown volumes is a low-cost remedy that exposes over-removal and allows public and judicial review (owner 09 adv §6, §10).
**Breakdown:**
- Benefit: exposes over-removal.
- Cost: low.
- Pairs with: safe-harbour incentive critique (IS-307).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 09 adv §6, §10 (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** IS-307, IS-314, IS-310.
**Trap:** Transparency reporting is not a substitute for due process.
**Mains hook:** Use as an "additional remedy" in the 2024 answer.
**Back:** *"Count the takedowns in public, not in private."*

### Flashcard IS-318 — Media literacy and verified-information channels (additional remedies)
**Front:** What additional non-legal remedies does the owner recommend for fake news?
**Definition (learn):** Media literacy, verified-information channels, community-level early-warning mechanisms and rapid official correction, which the owner calls preventive capabilities.
**Breakdown:**
- Media literacy campaigns (VisionIAS PDF p. 28).
- Verified-information channels (owner 09 §9).
- Community early-warning (owner 09 §9).
- Rapid official correction (owner 09 basic §12).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** VisionIAS PDF p. 28; owner 09 §9, §12 (✅ owner).
**Links:** IS-281, IS-313, IS-310.
**Trap:** Literacy is preventive, not a soft add-on, but it does not replace enforcement (owner §311 spine).
**Mains hook:** Use in the qualified conclusion.
**Back:** *"Literacy and verified voices: the preventive shield."*

### GROUP — 10 Terror Financing, Money Laundering and FATF (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard IS-319 — Money laundering: definition and the three stages
**Front:** Define money laundering and name its three stages.
**Definition (learn):** Money laundering is the process of making "dirty money" look like "clean money" (Singh PDF p. 91). It runs in three stages: placement, layering, integration.
**Breakdown:**
- Placement: dirty money enters the financial system (often cash deposits; riskiest stage).
- Layering: multiple transactions obscure origin.
- Integration: money re-enters the economy looking legitimate.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 91-92 (✅ owner 10 basic §1-§2).
**Links:** IS-320, IS-321, IS-322, IS-323, IS-324.
**Trap:** Laundering disguises the origin of already-acquired funds; it is not the same as providing funds for terror (IS-350).
**Mains hook:** Use the three-stage template as the spine for any laundering-channel answer; qualify that each stage has a different detection agency.
**Back:** *"Place, Layer, Integrate: the dirty becomes clean."*

### Flashcard IS-320 — Placement stage
**Front:** What happens at the placement stage?
**Definition (learn):** Inserting dirty money into a legitimate financial institution, often as cash deposits; the riskiest stage because large cash sums attract reporting requirements (Singh PDF p. 91).
**Breakdown:**
- Typical: cash deposits.
- Risk: triggers cash-transaction reporting.
- FIU-IND receives such reports (IS-331).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 91 (✅ owner 10 basic §2).
**Links:** IS-319, IS-331.
**Trap:** Placement is the stage most visible to reporting entities, not the most hidden.
**Mains hook:** Use to show why cash reporting matters; qualify that layering is harder to detect.
**Back:** *"Placement puts the cash on the counter."*

### Flashcard IS-321 — Layering stage
**Front:** What is layering and how is it done?
**Definition (learn):** Moving money through multiple transactions (bank-to-bank transfers, currency changes, high-value purchases) to obscure its origin (Singh PDF p. 91).
**Breakdown:**
- Methods: bank-to-bank transfers; currency conversions; high-value purchases.
- Aim: obscure origin.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 91 (✅ owner 10 basic §2).
**Links:** IS-319, IS-323, IS-340.
**Trap:** Layering is not the same as TBML; TBML is one layering channel (IS-323).
**Mains hook:** Use with channel-specific detection.
**Back:** *"Layering spins the trail."*

### Flashcard IS-322 — Integration stage
**Front:** What is integration?
**Definition (learn):** Money re-enters the mainstream economy in a legitimate-looking form and can be used without detection (Singh PDF p. 92).
**Breakdown:**
- Output: legitimate-looking funds.
- Risk: usable without detection.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 92 (✅ owner 10 basic §2).
**Links:** IS-319, IS-348.
**Trap:** Integration is the last stage; confiscation requires adjudication (IS-332).
**Mains hook:** Use to explain why late-stage detection is hard.
**Back:** *"Integration wears the clean coat."*

### Flashcard IS-323 — Trade-based money laundering (TBML) techniques
**Front:** What are the four TBML techniques and why is export over-invoicing common?
**Definition (learn):** FATF-defined process of disguising crime proceeds and moving value through trade transactions: over/under-invoicing, multiple-invoicing, over/under-shipment, falsely described goods or services (Singh PDF pp. 93-94).
**Breakdown:**
- Over/under-invoicing.
- Multiple-invoicing.
- Over/under-shipment.
- Falsely described goods/services.
- Over-invoicing exports is "one of the most common" because customs focus on import contraband, not export values (Singh PDF p. 93).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 93-94 (✅ owner 10 basic §2-§3).
**Links:** IS-321, IS-340, IS-341, IS-346.
**Trap:** TBML is not the same as virtual-asset laundering (IS-340). Customs' focus shift to export value is an institutional realignment.
**Mains hook:** "Explain how TBML works" (10 marks); qualify with customs priority shift.
**Back:** *"Over, under, multiple, false: four trade tricks."*

### Flashcard IS-324 — Prevention of Money-Laundering Act (PMLA), 2002
**Front:** What is the PMLA's enactment and commencement history?
**Definition (learn):** PMLA, 2002 is India's principal anti-money-laundering statute; it is Act No. 15 of 2003 (assent 17 January 2003 per Wikipedia fetched) and came into force on 1 July 2005.
**Breakdown:**
- Title year: 2002. Enactment: Act 15 of 2003. Commencement: 1 July 2005.
- Investigator: Enforcement Directorate (ED).
- Analyst: FIU-IND (s.12).
- Attachment power: s.5 (180 days; IS-332).
- Punishment: rigorous imprisonment 3-7 years; up to 10 years for NDPS-linked offences (Wikipedia fetched; s.4).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PMLA 2002 (Act 15 of 2003); 1 July 2005 (✅ Wikipedia fetched; ✅ owner 10 basic §2, §6).
**Links:** IS-325, IS-330, IS-331, IS-332, IS-339.
**Trap:** "PMLA, 2005" is wrong; 2005 is the commencement year. Owner trap confirmed by fetch.
**Mains hook:** Use the statute to anchor the mechanism; qualify with predicate dependency.
**Back:** *"Two-Thousand-Two titled, Two-Thousand-Five in force."*

### Flashcard IS-325 — PMLA as a derivative offence (predicate scheduled offence)
**Front:** Why is PMLA called a derivative offence?
**Definition (learn):** There is no money-laundering case without proceeds of crime from an offence listed in the PMLA Schedule and registered by the competent agency (owner 10 basic §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Predicate: scheduled offence.
- Gate: registered predicate case.
- Consequence: ED's caseload depends on other agencies (State police, CBI, NIA, NCB, customs) (adv §4).
- Scheduling is a policy lever.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PMLA Schedule (✅ owner 10 basic §2, adv §4; Wikipedia gives no count).
**Links:** IS-324, IS-342, IS-347.
**Trap:** ED cannot pursue laundering on its own initiative wherever it suspects illicit wealth (adv §8). Collapse of the predicate case defeats the laundering case.
**Mains hook:** "PMLA reach is set by predicate-offence quality" (15 marks); suggest reforms aimed at predicate investigation.
**Back:** *"No scheduled crime, no laundering case."*

### Flashcard IS-326 — UAPA Section 51A and the UN 1267 freezing route
**Front:** What does UAPA Section 51A require and how does it differ from PMLA?
**Definition (learn):** UAPA s.51A obliges the Government to freeze, seize or attach funds and financial assets of individuals and entities listed in connection with terrorism (including UN Security Council 1267 designations) and to prevent funds being made available to them.
**Breakdown:**
- Trigger: listing (not a predicate offence).
- Speed: faster and preventive.
- Nature: executive, not adjudicated; needs delisting/review safeguards (adv §4).
- Two channels: UAPA (fund-raising offences + s.51A) and PMLA (laundering of proceeds).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** UAPA 1967 s.51A; UNSC 1267 regime (✅ owner 10 basic §2, §4, adv §4). ⚠️ Section text not fetched.
**Links:** IS-325, IS-349, IS-327.
**Trap:** Terror-finance security is not only PMLA; s.51A works without a predicate case.
**Mains hook:** Name both channels; qualify that freezing is not conviction.
**Back:** *"Listed names freeze fast; laundering waits for the predicate."*

### Flashcard IS-327 — FATF (Financial Action Task Force)
**Front:** What is FATF, when was it founded, and what does it do?
**Definition (learn):** The global standard-setting body for AML/CFT; conducts Mutual Evaluations of member jurisdictions. Founded in 1989 at the G7 Summit in Paris (Wikipedia fetched); headquartered in Paris, secretariat hosted by the OECD.
**Breakdown:**
- Founded: 1989 (G7, Paris).
- Members: 40 (38 jurisdictions + 2 regional organisations; Wikipedia fetched).
- India: full member; owner says since 2010 (⚠️ Wikipedia lists membership without a joining date; 2010 not verified).
- Regional bodies: Asia/Pacific Group on Money Laundering; Eurasian Group (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** FATF 1989 (✅ Wikipedia fetched); India 2010 (⚠️ owner only).
**Links:** IS-328, IS-329, IS-350.
**Trap:** FATF is not a UN body; it is an intergovernmental standard-setter. Do not state India's 2010 date as verified.
**Mains hook:** Use to set the global standard context; qualify with the Mutual Evaluation outcome.
**Back:** *"Eighty-Nine in Paris, forty members, one standard."*

### Flashcard IS-328 — FATF listing categories vs follow-up categories
**Front:** Distinguish FATF grey list, black list and post-evaluation follow-up.
**Definition (learn):** Grey list = "jurisdictions under increased monitoring" (strategic deficiencies, action plan). Black list = "high-risk jurisdictions subject to a call for action" (formal name; Wikipedia fetched). Follow-up categories ("regular", "enhanced", "expedited enhanced") after a Mutual Evaluation describe reporting frequency, not deficiency status.
**Breakdown:**
- Grey list: increased monitoring.
- Black list: call for action (Iran, Myanmar, DPRK per Wikipedia as of Feb 2025).
- Follow-up: regular / enhanced reporting.
- Different processes.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** FATF lists since 2000 (✅ Wikipedia fetched; as of February 2025 list, time-varying).
**Links:** IS-327, IS-329.
**Trap:** "Grey list" and "regular follow-up" are not the same; "off the grey list" is not the same as "regular follow-up".
**Mains hook:** Use the distinction to show precision; qualify that list status is time-varying.
**Back:** *"Grey monitors, black calls, follow-up counts."*

### Flashcard IS-329 — FATF Mutual Evaluation of India (19 September 2024)
**Front:** What did FATF's India Mutual Evaluation Report (19 September 2024) conclude?
**Definition (learn):** India placed in "regular follow-up" (a reporting category, not a listing) with a progress report due to Plenary in 2027. FATF praised risk understanding, asset confiscation, beneficial-ownership transparency and JAM-enabled financial inclusion, while recommending faster conclusion of pending ML/TF prosecutions and risk-based non-profit outreach.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 19 September 2024 (owner; FATF page not fetched — 403/404).
- Category: regular follow-up.
- Report-back: 2027 (three years).
- Strengths: risk understanding, asset confiscation, beneficial ownership, JAM.
- Weaknesses named: prosecution speed; NPO outreach.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** FATF MER India, 19 Sept 2024 (📰 owner 10 basic §4, §8; ⚠️ not verified by fetch — FATF India page returned 403/404).
**Links:** IS-328, IS-348, IS-343, IS-341.
**Trap:** Do not present "regular follow-up" as an unqualified success; FATF named weaknesses. Do not assume the category is permanent.
**Mains hook:** "Assess FATF outcome" (15 marks): balance strengths and named recommendations.
**Back:** *"Two-Four-Nine-Nineteen: regular follow-up, prosecutions must speed."*

### Flashcard IS-330 — Enforcement Directorate (ED) and FEMA
**Front:** What does the ED do, and what does FEMA allow?
**Definition (learn):** ED investigates PMLA cases tied to a registered predicate offence, seeks provisional attachment, and also administers FEMA; FEMA contraventions can draw a penalty of up to three times the amount involved and confiscation of amounts held abroad (Singh PDF p. 96).
**Breakdown:**
- ED: PMLA investigation (criminal).
- FEMA: civil/adjudicatory penalty up to three times the amount.
- Confiscation of amounts held abroad (FEMA).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 96 (✅ owner 10 basic §4). ⚠️ FEMA penalty multiple not verified by fetch.
**Links:** IS-324, IS-331, IS-332.
**Trap:** FEMA is civil; PMLA is criminal. Do not conflate them.
**Mains hook:** Use to separate civil and criminal tools; qualify.
**Back:** *"ED prosecutes laundering; FEMA penalises exchange breaches."*

### Flashcard IS-331 — FIU-IND (Financial Intelligence Unit-India)
**Front:** What does FIU-IND do and where does it sit?
**Definition (learn):** FIU-IND receives suspicious transaction reports and cash transaction reports from financial entities under Section 12 of PMLA, analyses them and disseminates findings to enforcement and intelligence agencies. It functions under the Department of Revenue, Ministry of Finance.
**Breakdown:**
- Receives: STRs and CTRs (s.12 PMLA).
- Analyses and disseminates.
- Parent: Department of Revenue, MoF.
- Virtual digital asset service providers: brought within reporting obligations by notification (⚠️ verify scope).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PMLA s.12 (✅ owner 10 basic §4; Wikipedia fetched the reference only); MoF (✅ owner).
**Links:** IS-320, IS-324, IS-338.
**Trap:** FIU-IND analyses reports; it does not prosecute.
**Mains hook:** Use as the detection layer; qualify that detection is not conviction.
**Back:** *"Reports in, intelligence out, prosecution elsewhere."*

### Flashcard IS-332 — Four-step property ladder (attachment → adjudication → conviction → confiscation)
**Front:** Name the four steps of the property ladder and what each does.
**Definition (learn):** (i) ED makes a provisional attachment (s.5 PMLA, 180 days, per Wikipedia fetched); (ii) the Adjudicating Authority confirms or releases it; (iii) the Special Court convicts or acquits; (iv) only on conviction does property stand confiscated to the Central Government.
**Breakdown:**
- Step 1: provisional attachment (ED).
- Step 2: Adjudicating Authority confirmation.
- Step 3: Special Court trial.
- Step 4: confiscation after conviction.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PMLA s.5 (✅ Wikipedia fetched; ✅ owner 10 basic §3.5).
**Links:** IS-348, IS-324, IS-341.
**Trap:** "Assets attached" is a step-one statistic and does not show proven laundering. Attachment is not recovery.
**Mains hook:** The 20-mark attachment-vs-conviction answer; qualify with prosecution quality.
**Back:** *"Attach, adjudicate, convict, confiscate: never skip a rung."*

### Flashcard IS-333 — Terror-financing sources by threat type
**Front:** Compare the funding models of J&K, North-East and LWE.
**Definition (learn):** J&K militancy draws on state sponsorship (ISI), donations/charities via trusts, drug proceeds and diaspora/NGO funding; North-East insurgency relies on local extortion/taxation with drug, weapons and FICN trafficking; LWE relies overwhelmingly on local extortion from mining and infrastructure projects, with little external funding evidence (Singh PDF pp. 13-14).
**Breakdown:**
- J&K: external sponsorship; charities; drug proceeds.
- NE: extortion/taxation; drugs; weapons; FICN.
- LWE: local extortion; no substantive evidence of state sponsorship (Singh PDF p. 14).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 13-14 (✅ owner 10 basic §3.1, §7).
**Links:** IS-334, IS-343, topic 05, topic 04, topic 03, IS-346.
**Trap:** Not all terror funding is external state sponsorship (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** "Major terror funding sources" (15 marks); qualify that book-period groupings need current verification.
**Back:** *"Kashmir: state and trust. North-East: extortion and trade. Naxal: levy and mine."*

### Flashcard IS-334 — "Clean-clean" vs "dirty-clean" money
**Front:** Why is clean-clean money harder to detect than dirty-clean money?
**Definition (learn):** Clean-clean money is generated and transferred legally and "has certain legitimacy and no illegal hooks attached"; dirty-clean money is criminally generated and then laundered (Singh PDF p. 88).
**Breakdown:**
- Clean-clean: legal origin; no illegal marker.
- Dirty-clean: criminal origin; laundered.
- Consequence: sophisticated groups migrate to legitimate-looking businesses.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 88 (✅ owner 10 adv §2, §7).
**Links:** IS-319, IS-333, IS-350.
**Trap:** Clean-clean is not the same as black money (Economy owns general black-money tax mechanics; owner 10 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Use to explain why detection gets harder over time; qualify with the Economy boundary.
**Back:** *"Clean-clean carries no hook; dirty-clean carries the stain."*

### Flashcard IS-335 — Laundering as the enabling step for terror and drugs
**Front:** Why does laundering matter for terror and drug operations?
**Definition (learn):** Money laundering is "a crucial step in the success of drug trafficking and terrorist activities" because supporters do not write personal cheques and terrorists do not use credit cards or cheques for operational purchases (Singh PDF p. 91). Laundering breaks the traceable link between funder and act.
**Breakdown:**
- Breaks link: funder ↔ act.
- Relevance: drugs and terror.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 91 (✅ owner 10 basic §3.2).
**Links:** IS-319, IS-333, IS-346.
**Trap:** Laundering is distinct from providing funds for terror (IS-350).
**Mains hook:** Use to show why AML matters for CT.
**Back:** *"No cheque, no card: laundering hides the funder."*

### Flashcard IS-336 — WMD Act, 2005 (as amended 2022) — proliferation financing
**Front:** What does the 2022 amendment to the WMD Act, 2005 add?
**Definition (learn):** The Weapons of Mass Destruction and their Delivery Systems (Prohibition of Unlawful Activities) Act, 2005, as amended in 2022, prohibits financing of proliferation-related prohibited activity (owner 10 basic §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Act: WMD and Delivery Systems (Prohibition of Unlawful Activities) Act, 2005.
- Amendment: 2022.
- Covers: proliferation financing.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** WMD Act 2005 as amended 2022 (✅ owner 10 basic §4, §12). ⚠️ Amendment content not fetched.
**Links:** IS-326, IS-327.
**Trap:** Terror financing is not only PMLA or UAPA; WMD Act covers proliferation financing.
**Mains hook:** Use under global-standards compliance; qualify.
**Back:** *"Two-Thousand-Twenty-Two: proliferation finance is also finance."*

### Flashcard IS-337 — CrPC s.105A and BNSS 2023 (since 1 July 2024)
**Front:** Which provision governed foreign asset attachment, and what replaced it?
**Definition (learn):** Section 105A of the CrPC provided reciprocal arrangements for attachment and forfeiture of property abroad, executed through Letters Rogatory where a treaty exists (Singh PDF p. 96). Since 1 July 2024 the CrPC is replaced by the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023, which carries corresponding provisions.
**Breakdown:**
- Old: CrPC s.105A.
- New: BNSS 2023 (corresponding provisions; section number not verified).
- Commencement: 1 July 2024 (Wikipedia BNS page fetched for BNS commencement).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** CrPC s.105A; BNSS 2023 (✅ owner 10 basic §4; BNS commencement ✅ Wikipedia fetched).
**Links:** IS-267, IS-326.
**Trap:** Cite BNSS, not CrPC, for current provisions (owner).
**Mains hook:** Use under asset-recovery architecture; qualify with BNSS section.
**Back:** *"CrPC one-oh-five-A gives way to the BNSS."*

### Flashcard IS-338 — Virtual digital asset service providers under PMLA — ⚠️ status
**Front:** How are virtual digital asset service providers placed under PMLA?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 10 says VDA service providers have been brought within PMLA's reporting-entity obligations by notification; scope and registration must be verified from a dated FIU-IND/MoF release.
**Breakdown:**
- Status: by notification (owner).
- Consequence: reporting obligations.
- Verification: pending.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PMLA reporting-entity notification (⚠️ not verified; not fetched).
**Links:** IS-331, IS-340.
**Trap:** Do not state the scope or date without a dated release.
**Mains hook:** Use as an emerging channel; qualify.
**Back:** *"Crypto is in the net, but read the notification."*

### Flashcard IS-339 — Vijay Madanlal Choudhary v. Union of India (July 2022)
**Front:** What did Vijay Madanlal Choudhary (July 2022) uphold about PMLA?
**Definition (learn):** The Supreme Court upheld PMLA's core architecture, including ECIR's distinction from an FIR, ED officers' powers of arrest, search and recording statements under s.50, and the twin bail conditions in s.45 (owner adv §2). A review petition on some aspects was pending per owner.
**Breakdown:**
- Upheld: ECIR ≠ FIR.
- Upheld: ED powers under s.50 (statements).
- Upheld: s.45 twin bail conditions.
- Pending: review petition (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Vijay Madanlal Choudhary (July 2022) (✅ owner adv §2, §7; ⚠️ Wikipedia page not fetched — 404).
**Links:** IS-324, IS-330, IS-332.
**Trap:** The judgment upholds the regime; it does not say the regime is efficient. Conviction rate is the fair test (adv §2).
**Mains hook:** Use for "disruption vs conviction" trade-off; qualify with rights safeguards.
**Back:** *"Twenty-Twenty-Two upheld the arrest, the statement and the bail twin."*

### Flashcard IS-340 — TBML vs virtual-asset laundering (parallel channels)
**Front:** How do TBML and virtual-asset laundering differ?
**Definition (learn):** TBML exploits customs and trade-documentation gaps; virtual-asset laundering exploits decentralised, pseudonymous transaction infrastructure. Both need distinct detection tools.
**Breakdown:**
- TBML: trade documents; over/under-invoicing.
- VA: pseudonymity; decentralised infrastructure.
- Detection: different tools.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 10 adv §2, §6 (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** IS-323, IS-338, IS-341.
**Trap:** They are not the same detection problem (adv §8).
**Mains hook:** "Distinguish TBML from virtual-asset laundering" (15 marks); qualify with institutional readiness.
**Back:** *"Trade hides in invoices; crypto hides in pseudonyms."*

### Flashcard IS-341 — Detection, seizure and prosecution as three capability stages
**Front:** Why does FATF's 2024 critique point to prosecution as the weak link?
**Definition (learn):** India's strength lies in detection/risk understanding and asset seizure; the identified weakness is prosecution (slow conclusion of pending ML/TF cases) (owner adv §1, §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Detection: FIU-IND; risk understanding.
- Seizure: ED attachment.
- Prosecution: Special Courts; delay.
- Reform implication: invest in Special Court capacity, not only technology.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** FATF MER 2024 (📰 owner; not fetched).
**Links:** IS-329, IS-332, IS-348.
**Trap:** More surveillance technology addresses the wrong bottleneck (adv §3).
**Mains hook:** "Strong on detection, weak on prosecution" (15 marks); qualify with balance.
**Back:** *"Detect strong, seize strong, try slow."*

### Flashcard IS-342 — Predicate-offence dependency as a design feature
**Front:** Why does ED's reach depend on other agencies?
**Definition (learn):** Because PMLA requires a registered scheduled offence, ED's caseload is generated by State police, CBI, NIA, NCB and customs. Weak predicate registration or investigation caps the AML regime's reach; collapse of the predicate case is fatal to the laundering case.
**Breakdown:**
- Gate: registered predicate.
- Feeder agencies: State police, CBI, NIA, NCB, customs (owner adv §4).
- Consequence: reform must target predicate investigation.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PMLA Schedule (✅ owner 10 basic §2; ⚠️ agency list is owner's).
**Links:** IS-325, IS-347.
**Trap:** Reform aimed only at ED addresses the wrong link.
**Mains hook:** "PMLA reach is set by predicate quality" (15 marks).
**Back:** *"Predicate first, laundering second."*

### Flashcard IS-343 — Non-profit sector terror-financing risk and the Rabita Trust example
**Front:** Why does FATF recommend non-profit outreach, and what book example does Singh give?
**Definition (learn):** FATF's 2024 recommendation calls for risk-based non-profit outreach on terror-financing risk; charities have been used for cross-border fund movement, as in the book-period Al-Rashid/Rabita Trust example (Singh PDF p. 14, per owner adv §5).
**Breakdown:**
- Risk: charitable/non-profit channels.
- Example: Al-Rashid/Rabita Trust (book-period).
- Recommendation: risk-based outreach.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 14 (✅ owner adv §5); FATF 2024 (📰 owner).
**Links:** IS-329, IS-333.
**Trap:** Do not present a book-period group as a current funding network without fresh evidence.
**Mains hook:** Use under FATF recommendations; qualify.
**Back:** *"Charity can carry money across borders; outreach keeps it honest."*

### Flashcard IS-344 — JAM Trinity and formalisation (AML/CFT effect)
**Front:** How does JAM (Jan Dhan-Aadhaar-Mobile) relate to AML/CFT?
**Definition (learn):** FATF's 2024 praise cites JAM-enabled formalisation as reducing cash-based laundering opportunity (owner adv §5). The folder claims only this AML/CFT effect, not broader financial-inclusion policy.
**Breakdown:**
- JAM: Jan Dhan, Aadhaar, Mobile.
- AML/CFT effect: placement-stage opportunity reduced via formalisation.
- Economy owns broader inclusion merits.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** FATF 2024 (📰 owner; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** IS-320, IS-329.
**Trap:** Do not present JAM as a complete AML solution.
**Mains hook:** Use to show digital identity helps placement control; qualify.
**Back:** *"JAM formalises the cash; it does not catch the crook alone."*

### Flashcard IS-345 — PYQ 2021 GS-III Q9 (technology, globalisation and money laundering)
**Front:** What does the 2021 money laundering PYQ demand?
**Definition (learn):** "Discuss how emerging technologies and globalisation contribute to money laundering. Elaborate measures to tackle the problem of money laundering both at national and international levels." (Discuss · 10 marks · 150 words.)
**Breakdown:**
- Channels: rapid cross-border transfers; complex corporate ownership; TBML; virtual-asset service providers.
- Measures: FIU-IND, beneficial-owner scrutiny, PMLA obligations, FATF/international cooperation.
- Limit: monitoring without predicate investigation cannot deliver adjudicated outcomes.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2021 GS-III Q9 (✅ owner 10 basic §9, historical PYQ table).
**Links:** IS-323, IS-331, IS-338, IS-340.
**Trap:** Do not treat globalisation as an explanation by itself (owner Core §12).
**Mains hook:** Channel → control → limitation.
**Back:** *"Twenty-One's ten: channel, control, limit."*

### Flashcard IS-346 — PYQ 2023 GS-III Q20 (terror funding sources and FATF compliance, 15 marks)
**Front:** What does the 2023 terror-funding PYQ demand and how to answer?
**Definition (learn):** "Major terror funding sources in India and FATF compliance efforts" (Discuss · 15 marks · 250 words; owner routes Q20).
**Breakdown:**
- Sources: external sponsorship/donations/charities; extortion/parallel taxation; narcotics/FICN/organised crime; lawful-origin money diverted.
- Trace: reporting entity → FIU → investigation → PMLA/UAPA → court.
- FATF: balanced evaluation (IS-329).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2023 GS-III Q20 (✅ owner 10 basic §9, §12; historical PYQ table).
**Links:** IS-333, IS-326, IS-329, IS-332.
**Trap:** Do not present only the external-sponsorship source as the whole picture (IS-333).
**Mains hook:** Source comparison + mechanism + balanced FATF close.
**Back:** *"Twenty-Three's twenty: sources, trace, FATF balance."*

### Flashcard IS-347 — PMLA scheduled-offence count and MLAT count (book-period, ⚠️)
**Front:** What counts does Singh give for PMLA scheduled offences and MLATs, and what is their status?
**Definition (learn):** Singh (PDF pp. 94-95) gives 156 scheduled offences across 28 statutes and MLAT coverage with 26 countries. These are book-period figures; owner 10 requires verification from ED/MHA before citing.
**Breakdown:**
- Scheduled offences: 156 across 28 statutes (book-period).
- MLAT: 26 countries (book-period).
- Wikipedia fetched gives no count.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 94-95 (✅ owner 10 basic §4; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** IS-325, IS-342.
**Trap:** Do not state these numbers as current. Owner's trap explicitly warns.
**Mains hook:** Avoid numbers unless verified.
**Back:** *"One-fifty-six offences, twenty-six treaties: then, not now."*

### Flashcard IS-348 — Attachment versus conviction (disruption vs adjudicated outcome)
**Front:** What trade-off does "attachment without conviction" describe?
**Definition (learn):** A regime optimised for disruption shows strong attachment figures and weak conviction figures. This is not necessarily failure but must be argued as a deliberate trade-off (owner adv §2, §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Disruption: attachment, freezing, arrest now.
- Resolution: conviction and confiscation later.
- Risk: frozen assets with unresolved cases for years.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner adv §2, §6 (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** IS-332, IS-341, IS-322.
**Trap:** An attached asset is not a recovered asset (owner basic §7).
**Mains hook:** 20-mark attachment vs conviction answer; qualify with fair trial.
**Back:** *"Frozen is not forfeited: conviction decides the coin."*

### Flashcard IS-349 — Two channels against terror finance (PMLA and UAPA)
**Front:** Why must an answer name both UAPA and PMLA?
**Definition (learn):** Terror financing is attacked through two separate legal channels: UAPA (fund-raising offences and s.51A freezing) and PMLA (laundering of proceeds). Naming only one covers half the architecture.
**Breakdown:**
- UAPA: fund-raising offences; s.51A freezing.
- PMLA: laundering of proceeds; predicate offence.
- WMD Act 2005 (2022 amendment): proliferation financing.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 10 basic §2, §7.
**Links:** IS-326, IS-324, IS-336.
**Trap:** Terror financing is not prosecuted only under PMLA (owner basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Use to show architecture completeness.
**Back:** *"UAPA freezes the list; PMLA follows the proceeds."*

### Flashcard IS-350 — Money laundering vs terror financing (distinction)
**Front:** Distinguish money laundering from terror financing.
**Definition (learn):** Money laundering disguises the origin of already-acquired funds (often from crime); terror financing is the provision of funds, legitimately or illegitimately sourced, for a terrorist purpose. Related but analytically distinct.
**Breakdown:**
- Laundering: origin disguise.
- Terror financing: provision for purpose.
- Source may overlap; purpose differs.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 10 basic §7, §10 (✅).
**Links:** IS-319, IS-334, IS-335.
**Trap:** The two are not the same offence (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** "Distinguish money laundering from terror financing" (10 marks).
**Back:** *"Laundering hides the past; financing buys the future attack."*

### Flashcard IS-351 — Singh's global laundering-volume estimate (⚠️ book-period)
**Front:** What global laundering-volume figure does Singh give and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** Singh (PDF p. 92) estimates "$500 billion and $1 trillion worldwide every year." Owner 10 flags it as a book-period global estimate, not a current or India-specific figure.
**Breakdown:**
- Figure: $500 billion to $1 trillion per year (book-period).
- Status: do not cite as current.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 92 (✅ owner 10 basic §5; ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** IS-319, IS-347.
**Trap:** Do not quote as a current figure.
**Mains hook:** Avoid unless sourced.
**Back:** *"A trillion-dollar guess, dated and unverified."*

### GROUP — 11 Organised Crime, Narco-Terrorism and Trafficking (basic + advanced)

### Flashcard IS-352 — Organised crime: UNTOC-based definition
**Front:** Define organised crime and transnational organised crime.
**Definition (learn):** A structured group of three or more persons, existing over time, acting in concert to obtain financial or material benefit through serious crime (UN Convention definition, Singh PDF p. 83). Transnational organised crime adds a crime punishable by four or more years and cross-border reach (owner 11 basic §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Group: three or more persons.
- Time: existing over time.
- Aim: financial or material benefit.
- Offence threshold: serious crime (4+ years' punishment).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** UNTOC (Palermo Convention) (✅ owner 11 basic §2; Singh PDF p. 83).
**Links:** IS-353, IS-368, IS-356.
**Trap:** Organised crime is not terrorism; the objective differs (IS-356).
**Mains hook:** Use the UN definition as the anchor; qualify with national BNS definition (IS-353).
**Back:** *"Three or more, over time, for profit, serious crime."*

### Flashcard IS-353 — BNS Sections 111 and 112: organised crime and petty organised crime
**Front:** What do BNS Sections 111 and 112 create, and since when?
**Definition (learn):** Section 111, Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 defines organised crime (continuing unlawful activity by a syndicate, including kidnapping, extortion, contract killing, land grabbing, financial scams, cyber-crimes, trafficking); Section 112 creates the lesser offence of petty organised crime. In force from 1 July 2024.
**Breakdown:**
- s.111: organised crime by a syndicate.
- s.112: petty organised crime.
- Effect: first national general-law offence for organised crime.
- Start: 1 July 2024.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** BNS 2023 ss.111-112 (✅ owner 11 basic §2, §4, §6; BNS commencement 1 July 2024 ✅ Wikipedia fetched).
**Links:** IS-352, IS-367, IS-386, IS-354.
**Trap:** Before 1 July 2024 organised crime was not a national general-law offence; State special laws were the route. Do not date BNS 111 to 2023 commencement.
**Mains hook:** Use to show forum change; qualify (IS-386).
**Back:** *"One-Eleven syndicate, One-Twelve petty: general law, not only state law."*

### Flashcard IS-354 — BNS Section 113: terrorist act
**Front:** What does BNS Section 113 do and how does it sit with the IT Act?
**Definition (learn):** Section 113 of the BNS, 2023 defines the terrorist act offence in the general penal code (owner 11 basic §4). Cyber terrorism remains under IT Act s.66F (IS-243).
**Breakdown:**
- s.113: terrorist act (general penal code).
- Works alongside UAPA for terror offences (owner).
- Cyber terrorism: IT Act s.66F.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** BNS 2023 s.113 (✅ owner 11 basic §4; ⚠️ section text not fetched).
**Links:** IS-353, IS-243, IS-326.
**Trap:** Do not conflate BNS s.113 (terrorist act) with UAPA's definition. Verify section text before quoting.
**Mains hook:** Use to show general-law convergence of organised crime and terrorism; qualify.
**Back:** *"One-Thirteen names the terrorist act."*

### Flashcard IS-355 — BNS Sections 143-144: trafficking of persons and exploitation
**Front:** What do BNS Sections 143 and 144 cover and what IPC sections do they replace?
**Definition (learn):** Sections 143-144 cover trafficking of a person and exploitation of a trafficked person, replacing IPC Sections 370 and 370A (owner 11 basic §4).
**Breakdown:**
- s.143: trafficking of persons.
- s.144: exploitation of a trafficked person.
- Replaces: IPC 370 and 370A.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** BNS 2023 ss.143-144 (✅ owner 11 basic §4, §6; ⚠️ section text not fetched).
**Links:** IS-369, IS-385, IS-267.
**Trap:** For an offence after 1 July 2024 cite BNS, not IPC 370.
**Mains hook:** Use with the survivor-centred qualification (IS-388).
**Back:** *"One-Forty-Three traffics, One-Forty-Four exploits."*

### Flashcard IS-356 — Means-ends distinction: terrorism vs organised crime
**Front:** How do terrorism and organised crime differ in objective and method?
**Definition (learn):** Terrorism seeks to alter the status quo through political, ideological or religious goals using primarily violent means, based on support in oppressed communities. Organised crime seeks to expand markets and form a "parallel government" or "parallel economy" while co-existing with the existing order, preferring non-violence "notwithstanding the odd resort to belligerence" (Singh PDF pp. 84-85).
**Breakdown:**
- Terror: political objective; violence; status-quo altering.
- Organised crime: economic objective; non-violence preferred; parallel economy.
- They form a nexus, not an identity.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 84-85 (✅ owner 11 basic §1, §3.1).
**Links:** IS-357, IS-378, IS-352.
**Trap:** "Organised crime = terrorism" is the top trap (owner basic §7). Open any narco-terror answer with this distinction.
**Mains hook:** "Distinguish organised crime from terrorism" (10 marks); conclude with convergence in narco-terrorism.
**Back:** *"Terror wants the throne; crime wants the market."*

### Flashcard IS-357 — Narco-terrorism (definition and convergence)
**Front:** Define narco-terrorism.
**Definition (learn):** The convergence of narcotics trafficking with terrorism or insurgent financing: armed groups tax, control or profit from drug cultivation and trafficking routes to fund political violence.
**Breakdown:**
- Convergence: drug trafficking + insurgent/terror finance.
- Mechanism: taxation, control of routes, direct profit.
- Not every narcotics case is terrorism.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 11 basic §2, §12 (✅ owner; analytical definition).
**Links:** IS-356, IS-365, IS-358, IS-377.
**Trap:** Narco-terrorism is not simply drug-addiction policy (owner basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Define first, then route, region and response.
**Back:** *"Narcotics meet terror where the money changes hands."*

### Flashcard IS-358 — NDPS Act, 1985 (principal narcotics statute)
**Front:** What does the NDPS Act, 1985 do and when was it enacted?
**Definition (learn):** The Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances Act, 1985 is India's principal narcotics statute; it prohibits production, possession, sale and transport of narcotic drugs and psychotropic substances except for medical or scientific purposes, and grades punishment by quantity. Enacted 1985 (assent 16 September 1985; commenced 14 November 1985, per Wikipedia fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Prohibits: production, manufacture, possession, sale, transport, consumption (except medical/scientific).
- Grading: small, between small and commercial, commercial quantity (IS-359).
- Amended 2014 to strengthen forfeiture (Wikipedia fetched).
- Has 6 chapters and 83 sections (Wikipedia fetched).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NDPS Act 1985 (✅ Wikipedia fetched; ✅ owner 11 basic §2, §4).
**Links:** IS-359, IS-360, IS-361, IS-383, IS-384.
**Trap:** Do not state the Chapter VA or NCB section number as verified; Wikipedia fetch did not confirm those sections (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Name NDPS in every narco-terror answer; qualify with treatment and demand-side provisions.
**Back:** *"Eighty-Five prohibits, grades, and forfeits."*

### Flashcard IS-359 — NDPS quantity thresholds and penalty tiers
**Front:** What penalty bands and quantity thresholds apply under NDPS?
**Definition (learn):** Small quantity: rigorous imprisonment up to 1 year, fine up to ₹10,000, or both. Between small and commercial: up to 10 years and fine up to ₹1 lakh. Commercial quantity: 10 to 20 years and fine ₹1 lakh to ₹2 lakh (Wikipedia fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Small: up to 1 year.
- Intermediate: up to 10 years.
- Commercial: 10-20 years.
- Examples (fetched): heroin 5 g / 250 g; ganja 1 kg / 20 kg; charas 100 g / 1 kg; cocaine 2 g / 100 g (small / commercial).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NDPS Act 1985 (✅ Wikipedia fetched; statutory schedule not checked against India Code).
**Links:** IS-358, IS-383.
**Trap:** The threshold table is a Schedule matter; quote the schedule, not memory, in a precise answer.
**Mains hook:** Use quantity grading to show proportionate punishment; qualify with the treatment provision.
**Back:** *"Small one year, commercial twenty, the grams decide the fate."*

### Flashcard IS-360 — PIT-NDPS Act, 1988 (preventive detention)
**Front:** What does the PIT-NDPS Act, 1988 provide?
**Definition (learn):** The Prevention of Illicit Trafficking in Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances Act, 1988 permits preventive detention of traffickers (owner 11 basic §2, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Type: preventive detention.
- Target: traffickers.
- Companion to NDPS Act 1985.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PIT-NDPS Act 1988 (✅ Wikipedia infobox reference; ✅ owner).
**Links:** IS-358, IS-324.
**Trap:** Preventive detention is not a conviction; do not describe it as punishment.
**Mains hook:** Use under the "law" layer; qualify with liberty safeguards.
**Back:** *"Eighty-Eight holds the trafficker before proof."*

### Flashcard IS-361 — Narcotics Control Bureau (NCB)
**Front:** What is the NCB, under which ministry, and what does it coordinate?
**Definition (learn):** The NCB is the central nodal drug-law enforcement agency under MHA; it coordinates multi-agency counter-narcotic action and international partnerships (Interpol, Navy, border forces). Effective March 1986 (Wikipedia fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Parent: MHA.
- Status: central nodal agency.
- Partners: Interpol, Navy, border forces.
- Expanded mandate: crypto/darknet/drone intelligence; zonal offices (owner adv §4).
- Section 4(3) NDPS reference ⚠️ not verified.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NCB under NDPS Act; effective March 1986 (✅ Wikipedia fetched; 📰 owner adv §4).
**Links:** IS-358, IS-362, IS-363, IS-364.
**Trap:** NCB is a nodal central agency; ANTF is a State/UT unit; NCORD is a coordination mechanism. Do not swap them.
**Mains hook:** Cite NCB by precise role (owner basic §10).
**Back:** *"NCB nodes the nation, ANTF runs the State."*

### Flashcard IS-362 — NCORD (National Narcotics Coordination) — four tiers
**Front:** What are the four tiers of NCORD?
**Definition (learn):** NCORD is a four-tier mechanism (Apex, Executive, State, District) coordinating drug-law enforcement across MHA, NCB and State/district agencies, precursor-diversion control, demand reduction and rehabilitation (owner 11 basic §3.5, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Tier 1: Apex.
- Tier 2: Executive.
- Tier 3: State.
- Tier 4: District.
- Addresses the Centre-State coordination problem (adv §4).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NCORD (📰 owner 11 basic §4, adv §4; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** IS-361, IS-363, IS-364.
**Trap:** NCORD is coordination, not operational command.
**Mains hook:** Use as coordinated response; qualify with State implementation.
**Back:** *"Apex, Executive, State, District: four floors of coordination."*

### Flashcard IS-363 — Anti-Narcotics Task Force (ANTF) — State/UT units
**Front:** What is the ANTF and what is its priority?
**Definition (learn):** Every State and UT has a mandatory Anti-Narcotics Task Force acting as NCORD's local secretariat, prioritising financial investigation (tracing and disrupting narcotics-related money flows) and linking local cases to interstate and transnational cartel activity (owner 11 basic §3.5, adv §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Coverage: every State/UT.
- Role: local secretariat; financial investigation.
- Link: interstate and transnational cartels.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** ANTF (📰 owner 11 basic §4, adv §4; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** IS-362, IS-361, IS-384.
**Trap:** ANTF is State/UT-level, not a central agency.
**Mains hook:** Use to show financial-investigation mandate; qualify with capacity.
**Back:** *"ANTF follows the money in every State."*

### Flashcard IS-364 — MHA Vision Document on Drug Control (2026-2029), June 2026
**Front:** What is the 2026-2029 Vision Document and what are its three verbs and goal?
**Definition (learn):** Released June 2026 by MHA; zero-tolerance framework organised around "Detect, Disrupt, Destroy," covering supply reduction, demand reduction and harm reduction, with explicit high-tech focus (darknet, cryptocurrency payments, drone trafficking, maritime smuggling) and a stated "drug-free Bharat by 2047" goal.
**Breakdown:**
- Verbs: Detect; Disrupt; Destroy.
- Pillars: supply, demand, harm reduction.
- Tech focus: darknet, crypto, drones, maritime.
- Goal: drug-free Bharat by 2047 (stated goal, not achievement).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** MHA Vision Document 2026-2029 (📰 owner 11 basic §4, §8; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** IS-361, IS-362, IS-363, IS-365, IS-383.
**Trap:** A goal is not a measured outcome (owner adv §6).
**Mains hook:** Use as the most recent counter-measure layer; qualify with dated progress.
**Back:** *"Detect, Disrupt, Destroy: a goal in 2047, not a result in 2026."*

### Flashcard IS-365 — Golden Crescent and Golden Triangle (heroin routes)
**Front:** Which two regions are the principal heroin source routes into Asia-Pacific including India?
**Definition (learn):** The Golden Crescent (Afghanistan, Iran, Pakistan) and the Golden Triangle (Myanmar, Laos, Thailand) are the two principal heroin-source regions feeding narcotics into the Asia-Pacific, including India (Singh PDF p. 89).
**Breakdown:**
- Golden Crescent: Afghanistan, Iran, Pakistan.
- Golden Triangle: Myanmar, Laos, Thailand.
- Route to India: major transit routes.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 89 (✅ owner 11 basic §2, §6).
**Links:** IS-357, IS-381, IS-364.
**Trap:** Do not place Afghanistan in the Golden Triangle or Myanmar in the Golden Crescent.
**Mains hook:** Use with route-geography as policy lever.
**Back:** *"Crescent: Afghanistan, Iran, Pakistan. Triangle: Myanmar, Laos, Thailand."*

### Flashcard IS-366 — FICN as organised-crime and terror-finance tool
**Front:** Why is FICN a named organised-crime tool?
**Definition (learn):** Fake Indian Currency Notes are an organised-crime and terror-financing tool used to fund militancy and destabilise the economy. Owner says FICN is a scheduled offence under the NIA Act and, since 2012, within UAPA's expanded terrorist act.
**Breakdown:**
- Use: fund militancy; destabilise economy.
- NIA Act: scheduled offence (owner).
- UAPA: expanded "terrorist act" since 2012 (owner; ⚠️ year not verified).
- Kashmir: FICN circulation (Singh PDF pp. 85-87).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NIA Act 2008 schedule; UAPA 2012 amendment (📰 owner; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** IS-370, IS-326, IS-333.
**Trap:** FICN is distinct from narcotics and arms; do not merge them into one trafficking category.
**Mains hook:** Use under terror-finance; qualify with verification.
**Back:** *"Fake notes fund the gun, not the market."*

### Flashcard IS-367 — MCOCA, 1999 (Maharashtra control of organised crime)
**Front:** What are the key MCOCA provisions?
**Definition (learn):** Maharashtra Control of Organised Crime Act, 1999 (Mah. 30 of 1999; began as an ordinance on 24 February 1999). Confessions to police officers of Superintendent rank or higher are admissible, including against co-conspirators; anticipatory bail is unavailable; ordinary bail is stricter; detention is extended up to 180 days; Special Courts may shield witness identities (Wikipedia fetched).
**Breakdown:**
- Confessions: SP-rank and above; admissible against co-accused (owner also).
- Bail: no anticipatory bail; stricter ordinary bail.
- Extended detention: from 30 days up to 180 days (Wikipedia fetched).
- Witness protection: identity secrecy; sealed orders; barred publication; penalty up to 1 year and ₹1,000.
- ⚠️ Owner says "180 days (rather than 90) to file a charge-sheet": Wikipedia describes 30 to 180 extended detention; reconcile before quoting.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** MCOCA 1999 (✅ Wikipedia fetched; ✅ owner 11 basic §4).
**Links:** IS-353, IS-386, IS-389.
**Trap:** MCOCA is a State law; BNS 111 is national general law. Do not state the 90-day figure without verification.
**Mains hook:** "Forum changes, difficulty remains" (15 marks); qualify with procedural advantages.
**Back:** *"Ninety-Nine, Superintendent confesses, one-eighty holds."*

### Flashcard IS-368 — UN Convention against Transnational Organised Crime (UNTOC, Palermo) and three Protocols
**Front:** What is UNTOC and what are its three Protocols?
**Definition (learn):** The UN Convention against Transnational Organised Crime (Palermo Convention) is described by Singh as "the only international convention which deals with organised crime"; its three Protocols cover trafficking in persons, smuggling of migrants and trafficking of firearms (Singh PDF p. 90). India is a party (owner 11 basic §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Convention: UNTOC (Palermo).
- Protocols: trafficking in persons; smuggling of migrants; trafficking of firearms.
- India: party (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** UNTOC (✅ owner 11 basic §2, §4, §6; Singh PDF p. 90).
**Links:** IS-352, IS-355, IS-369.
**Trap:** Smuggling of migrants and trafficking in persons are legally distinct (owner Core §12).
**Mains hook:** Use international cooperation layer; qualify with Interpol and MLAT.
**Back:** *"Palermo: one convention, three protocols, three crimes."*

### Flashcard IS-369 — Article 23, ITPA 1956, Bonded Labour Act 1976 and AHTUs
**Front:** Which constitutional and statutory anchors fight human trafficking, and what is an AHTU?
**Definition (learn):** Article 23 of the Constitution prohibits traffic in human beings and forced labour; the Immoral Traffic (Prevention) Act, 1956 and the Bonded Labour System (Abolition) Act, 1976 supplement BNS 143-144; MHA supports State/UT Anti-Human Trafficking Units (AHTUs) as district-level investigative mechanisms (owner 11 basic §4, §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Constitution: Article 23.
- Statutes: ITPA 1956; Bonded Labour Act 1976; BNS 143-144.
- Units: AHTUs (district-level).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Article 23; ITPA 1956; Bonded Labour Act 1976; AHTUs (📰 owner; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** IS-355, IS-368, IS-385.
**Trap:** AHTUs are investigative units, not social-welfare bodies.
**Mains hook:** Pair with survivor-centred protection (IS-388).
**Back:** *"Article Twenty-Three forbids the trade; AHTUs chase it."*

### Flashcard IS-370 — NIA Act, 2008 — schedule covering organised crime, FICN, trafficking, narcotics
**Front:** Which offences does the NIA Act schedule cover?
**Definition (learn):** The NIA Act, 2008 covers organised crime (extortion gangs), terrorism, FICN, human trafficking and narcotics within its schedule, bringing these under federal investigative jurisdiction when linked to the schedule (owner 11 basic §4, adv §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Schedule: terrorism, FICN, human trafficking, narcotics, organised crime (extortion mobs and gangs).
- Federal investigation jurisdiction.
- Topic 12 develops NIA in full.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NIA Act, 2008 (✅ owner 11 basic §4; ⚠️ schedule not verified).
**Links:** IS-366, IS-371, IS-385, topic 12.
**Trap:** NIA schedule ≠ every organised-crime case; linkage matters.
**Mains hook:** Use to show central-State layering.
**Back:** *"Two-Thousand-Eight schedules the serious: terror, notes, people, drugs."*

### Flashcard IS-371 — Arms Act, 1959 (amended 2019) and Explosive Substances Act, 1908
**Front:** Which statutes govern illicit-arms trafficking and explosives?
**Definition (learn):** Arms Act, 1959 (amended 2019) and Explosive Substances Act, 1908 are the statutes behind illicit-arms cases; offences relating to prohibited arms and explosive substances became NIA-scheduled after the 2019 amendment (owner 11 basic §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Arms Act 1959, amended 2019.
- Explosive Substances Act 1908.
- Post-2019: NIA-scheduled offences (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Arms Act 1959 (amended 2019); Explosive Substances Act 1908 (✅ owner; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** IS-370, IS-368, IS-352.
**Trap:** Do not present NIA-schedule status without linked case.
**Mains hook:** Use under arms-trafficking; qualify.
**Back:** *"Fifty-Nine arms, Eight explosives: NIA-listed after 2019."*

### Flashcard IS-372 — Dawood Ibrahim / D-Company (book-period case)
**Front:** Why is the D-Company case used in organised crime-terror analysis, and what is its status caveat?
**Definition (learn):** Singh (PDF pp. 87-88) describes Dawood Ibrahim's D-Company and links it to the 1993 Bombay blasts as a historical organised crime-terror nexus case. Status claims (Interpol listing, 2003 US designation, Forbes ranking) are book-period.
**Breakdown:**
- Nexus: organised crime to 1993 blasts.
- Book-period status: not current.
- Verify current status from NIA/NCB/Interpol.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 87-88 (✅ owner 11 basic §5; adv §5).
**Links:** IS-224, IS-356, IS-357.
**Trap:** Do not present the individual's present status as current.
**Mains hook:** Use as historical case study only.
**Back:** *"Historic syndicate, dated status: verify before you name."*

### Flashcard IS-373 — INTERPOL notice categories (Red, Blue, Green, Silver and others)
**Front:** What are the INTERPOL notice colours in the owner's routed Prelims safety block?
**Definition (learn):** Silver: identify and trace criminal assets; Blue: obtain additional information on identity, location or activities; Black: seek information on unidentified bodies; Green: warn about criminal activities or public-safety risk. These are cooperation notices or requests, not convictions or arrest warrants (owner 11 Core §12).
**Breakdown:**
- Silver: asset tracing (⚠️ check; Silver is not in the older Interpol colour list in Wikipedia-era memory).
- Blue: identity, location, activities.
- Black: unidentified bodies.
- Green: warning.
- Red: arrest request (⚠️ owner does not list; from memory).
- Yellow: missing persons; Orange: warning/event; Purple: modus operandi (⚠️ memory, not fetched).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** INTERPOL notices (✅ owner 11 Core §12; ⚠️ Wikipedia fetch returned 404; Red/Yellow/Orange/Purple from memory).
**Links:** IS-374, IS-368.
**Trap:** A notice is not a warrant. Red is the arrest-request notice (⚠️ memory); do not present a key letter.
**Mains hook:** Prelims statement-level only; not a Mains point.
**Back:** *"Silver traces assets, Blue asks, Black seeks bodies, Green warns."*

### Flashcard IS-374 — PYQ 2026 Prelims GS-I Q76 (INTERPOL notice categories) — provisional key
**Front:** What does the 2026 Prelims Q76 test, and what is its key status?
**Definition (learn):** Routed demand: "INTERPOL notice categories and their distinct international investigative purposes" (Objective question). The local 2026 Set-A key is provisional; no option letter is recorded or inferred.
**Breakdown:**
- Test: statement-level distinctions between notice colours.
- Key status: provisional; not inferred.
- Use: learn definitions (IS-373).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2026 Prelims GS-I Q76 (✅ owner 11 2026 PYQ block; `_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2026.md`).
**Links:** IS-373.
**Trap:** Do not state an answer letter as official.
**Mains hook:** Not applicable (Prelims).
**Back:** *"Seventy-Six tests the colours; the key is still provisional."*

### Flashcard IS-375 — PYQ 2018 GS-III Q20 (drug trafficking, money laundering and human trafficking)
**Front:** What does the 2018 GS-III PYQ demand and what is its Core route?
**Definition (learn):** "Explain the linkages between drug trafficking with money laundering and human trafficking" (paraphrased owner routing: drug trafficking linkages with money laundering and human trafficking; Explain · 15 marks · 250 words).
**Breakdown:**
- Network chain: source/transit → recruiter or cartel → transport/documents/corruption → exploitation/proceeds → laundering.
- Legal legs: NDPS/NCB/NCORD; BNS 143-144 and AHTUs; PMLA/FIU/ED.
- Close: victim protection, not an enforcement metric.
