**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2018 GS-III Q20 (✅ owner 11 basic §12; historical PYQ table).
**Links:** IS-355, IS-358, IS-369, IS-324, IS-388.
**Trap:** Human trafficking is not only a social-welfare issue (owner basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Network diagram + legal legs + survivor qualification.
**Back:** *"Eighteen's fifteen: route, legs, survivors."*

### Flashcard IS-376 — PYQ 2022 GS-III Q9 (organised crime types and terror linkages)
**Front:** What does the 2022 GS-III PYQ demand?
**Definition (learn):** "Discuss the types of organised crimes. Describe the linkages between terrorists and organised crime that exist at the national and transnational levels." (Discuss · 10 marks · 150 words.)
**Breakdown:**
- Classify types first: narcotics, arms, human trafficking, FICN, extortion, contract killing, maritime piracy, cyber crime.
- Four shared links: finance, logistics, weapons/technology, territorial/corrupt protection.
- National and transnational scale; UNTOC/Interpol responses.
- Close: seizure is not a network outcome.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2022 GS-III Q9 (✅ owner 11 basic §9, §12).
**Links:** IS-352, IS-356, IS-378, IS-368.
**Trap:** Listing types without linkages scores low.
**Mains hook:** Classify → four links → two scales → qualified close.
**Back:** *"Twenty-Two's ten: classify, link, scale, qualify."*

### Flashcard IS-377 — PYQ 2024 GS-III Q9 (narco-terrorism as threat and counter-measures)
**Front:** What does the 2024 narco-terrorism PYQ demand?
**Definition (learn):** "Explain how narco-terrorism has emerged as a serious threat across the country. Suggest suitable measures to counter narco-terrorism." (Explain and suggest · 10 marks · 150 words per owner routing; owner's basic verbatim line.)
**Breakdown:**
- Define nexus; means-ends distinction.
- Route geography: Golden Crescent/Triangle.
- Regional variation: NE extortion; J&K externally enabled; other route networks.
- Legal architecture: NDPS (Chapter VA ⚠️), PIT-NDPS, BNS 111-113, NIA schedule, PMLA where narcotics is predicate.
- Institutions: NCORD, ANTF, NCB, 2026 Vision Document.
- Close: seizures are inputs, not proof of outcome.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2024 GS-III Q9 (✅ owner 11 basic §9, §12; routed ledger `_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-GS3-GS4-2024-2025.md`).
**Links:** IS-357, IS-365, IS-379, IS-358, IS-361, IS-362, IS-363, IS-364.
**Trap:** Mark mismatch: basic §9 gives Q9 with 150 words, the other owner table gives 10 marks. Use the paper wording and say "10 marks" consistent with owner Core §12.
**Mains hook:** Regional-model analysis is the analytical core (adv §10).
**Back:** *"Twenty-Four's narco-terror: define, route, region, law, institution, verdict."*

### Flashcard IS-378 — Nexus mechanism: shared networks and protection arrangements
**Front:** How do organised crime and terror groups link operationally?
**Definition (learn):** Organised crime groups and terrorists often operate on the same network structures and weak-governance territory; criminal groups provide smuggled arms and explosives to terror groups in exchange for drugs; terror groups controlling territory tax drug traffickers for protection (Singh PDF pp. 84-85).
**Breakdown:**
- Shared: networks; weak-governance terrain.
- Exchange: arms/explosives for drugs.
- Protection: tax for route control.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 84-85 (✅ owner 11 basic §3.2).
**Links:** IS-356, IS-357, IS-376.
**Trap:** A nexus is not identity; the ends differ.
**Mains hook:** Use under "linkages" answers.
**Back:** *"Arms for drugs, tax for passage: the nexus trades."*

### Flashcard IS-379 — Regional variation of the nexus in India (NE, J&K, rest of India)
**Front:** How does the nexus differ across regions?
**Definition (learn):** North-East: militant groups run a "parallel government", collecting money and diverting government contracts. J&K: external funds (via organisations such as Markaz-dawa-al-Arshad, Pakistan/Gulf) substitute for weak internal extortion, supplemented by FICN. Rest of India: sporadic, network-based (owner adv §2, §7).
**Breakdown:**
- North-East: parallel government; extortion; diversion of contracts.
- J&K: external and diaspora funds; FICN.
- Rest of India: sporadic; Mumbai-blast style.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 85-87 (✅ owner 11 basic §3.3, adv §2).
**Links:** IS-333, IS-380, IS-377.
**Trap:** A uniform strategy under-serves regional models (adv §6).
**Mains hook:** Core analytical axis for "not uniform" answers.
**Back:** *"North-East taxes, Kashmir imports, the rest networks."*

### Flashcard IS-380 — Structural vs sponsored funding (LWE vs J&K)
**Front:** Why does the funding distinction determine the counter-measure?
**Definition (learn):** LWE relies on structural extortion from infrastructure and mining projects with no substantive evidence of state sponsorship; J&K's model is externally sponsored or diaspora-linked. The first calls for domestic asset-tracing; the second for cross-border financial-intelligence cooperation (owner adv §2).
**Breakdown:**
- LWE: structural extortion; domestic asset-tracing.
- J&K: external sponsorship; cross-border intelligence.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 13-14 (✅ owner adv §2).
**Links:** IS-333, IS-379, topic 03.
**Trap:** Do not extend the J&K model to LWE.
**Mains hook:** Use for calibrated counter-measures.
**Back:** *"Extortion needs local tracing; sponsorship needs foreign tracing."*

### Flashcard IS-381 — Route geography as a separate policy lever
**Front:** Why is transit-infrastructure interdiction a lever distinct from group enforcement?
**Definition (learn):** Disrupting transit infrastructure (border checkpoints, maritime chokepoints, hawala corridors) is a policy lever independent of, and complementary to, disrupting any group's organisational capacity (owner adv §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Targets: transit corridors, chokepoints, hawala.
- Complementary to group enforcement.
- Maritime link: topic 07.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 89 (✅ owner adv §2; ⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** IS-365, IS-357, IS-241.
**Trap:** Seizure at a checkpoint is not network disruption.
**Mains hook:** Use in route-based answers.
**Back:** *"Watch the road, not only the gang."*

### Flashcard IS-382 — Supply, demand and harm reduction: three policy logics
**Front:** Distinguish the three drug-policy logics and their conflict.
**Definition (learn):** Supply reduction is enforcement-led (interdiction, forfeiture, cartel dismantling); demand reduction is education and community-led; harm reduction is public-health-led (treatment, de-addiction, overdose management). They can conflict, which is why NDPS has a treatment-in-lieu-of-prosecution provision for addicts (owner adv §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Supply: enforcement.
- Demand: education and community.
- Harm: health and treatment.
- Tension: criminalising users deters treatment.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner adv §2 (⚠️ analytical; NDPS treatment provision not fetched).
**Links:** IS-364, IS-358.
**Trap:** Treating users as security targets misreads the policy design.
**Mains hook:** Use under counter-narcotics answer; qualify.
**Back:** *"Supply stops, demand learns, harm heals."*

### Flashcard IS-383 — Seizure and destruction are not outcomes (trap)
**Front:** Why are seizure or destruction volumes not proof of reduced trafficking?
**Definition (learn):** Seizure and destruction measure enforcement activity. Supply reduction is evidenced by price, purity and availability data, and by conviction and forfeiture outcomes under NDPS, not by tonnage (owner basic §7, adv §8).
**Breakdown:**
- Activity measures: seizures, destruction.
- Outcome measures: prices, purity, availability, convictions, forfeiture.
- Verification: dated NCB/MHA releases.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner basic §7, adv §8, §9 (✅ owner analytical).
**Links:** IS-358, IS-364, IS-332.
**Trap:** Large seizures can reflect more production or more enforcement; do not read them as direction.
**Mains hook:** Use to qualify any "measures" answer.
**Back:** *"Tonnes measure the net, not the fish."*

### Flashcard IS-384 — Two-track property attack (NDPS Chapter VA and PMLA)
**Front:** What are the two property tracks against traffickers?
**Definition (learn):** NDPS Chapter VA (Sections 68A-68F, per owner) provides forfeiture of illegally acquired property; PMLA allows attachment where an NDPS offence is the scheduled predicate (topic 10). Both need parallel financial investigation (owner basic §4, adv §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Track 1: NDPS forfeiture (penal action vs proceeds).
- Track 2: PMLA attachment (predicate: NDPS).
- Parallel: financial investigation (ANTF mandate).
- ⚠️ Chapter VA and 68A-68F section numbers not verified by fetch.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NDPS 1985 Chapter VA (⚠️ owner; not verified); PMLA (✅ Wikipedia fetched s.5 attachment).
**Links:** IS-358, IS-324, IS-332, IS-363.
**Trap:** Seizure is not forfeiture; provisional attachment is not confiscation.
**Mains hook:** Concrete content of "follow the money".
**Back:** *"Two tracks: forfeit under NDPS, attach under PMLA."*

### Flashcard IS-385 — Human trafficking: scheduled offence and federal route
**Front:** Why is human trafficking both a BNS offence and a federal matter?
**Definition (learn):** Human trafficking is a BNS offence (ss.143-144), a scheduled offence under the NIA Act (owner, ⚠️), and shares routes and facilitators with narcotics and arms trafficking; AHTUs investigate at district level (owner basic §4, §7).
**Breakdown:**
- BNS 143-144.
- NIA Act schedule (⚠️).
- AHTUs.
- Shared routes with drugs and arms.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** BNS 2023; NIA Act 2008; AHTUs (✅ owner; ⚠️ NIA schedule not verified).
**Links:** IS-355, IS-369, IS-370.
**Trap:** Human trafficking is not only a women-and-child welfare issue (owner basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Use to show security and rights dimensions.
**Back:** *"Traffic is crime and security, not only welfare."*

### Flashcard IS-386 — BNS 111 displaces state laws or runs alongside them? (live issue)
**Front:** What are the three live analytical issues after BNS 111-112?
**Definition (learn):** (i) whether BNS 111 will displace State special laws (MCOCA-type) in practice or run alongside them; (ii) whether the procedural advantages (confession admissibility, bail stringency) survive under general-law procedure; (iii) whether uniform national coverage reduces inter-State forum-shopping (owner adv §4). None is settled.
**Breakdown:**
- Issue 1: displacement vs coexistence.
- Issue 2: procedural survival.
- Issue 3: forum-shopping.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner adv §4, §12 (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** IS-353, IS-367.
**Trap:** Do not claim BNS 111 has replaced MCOCA; the owner says the question is open.
**Mains hook:** "Changes the forum, not the difficulty" (15 marks).
**Back:** *"Forum changes, procedure tests, shopping ends?"*

### Flashcard IS-387 — Darknet, cryptocurrency and drone trafficking (technological frontier)
**Front:** Why does the 2026 Vision Document focus on darknet, crypto and drones?
**Definition (learn):** Trafficking has adapted to darknet markets, cryptocurrency payments, drone-borne smuggling and maritime routes; conventional route interdiction is insufficient alone (owner adv §3, §5, §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Darknet: anonymous markets.
- Crypto: payment channel.
- Drones: aerial delivery.
- Response: NCB technology-led detection; digital forensics capacity.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** MHA Vision Document 2026-2029 (📰 owner; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** IS-364, IS-338, IS-340, IS-361.
**Trap:** Darknet is an S&T-adjacent technical domain; this topic claims only the institutional response (owner adv §5).
**Mains hook:** "Critically examine readiness" (15 marks); qualify with capacity gap.
**Back:** *"Dark web, crypto coins, drone flights: the newest road."*

### Flashcard IS-388 — Survivor-centred qualification (victim protection is not an enforcement metric)
**Front:** Why must trafficking answers include survivor protection?
**Definition (learn):** Victim protection and rehabilitation cannot be reduced to an enforcement metric; trafficked people should not be treated as criminal facilitators (owner basic §12 decoder, 20-mark route).
**Breakdown:**
- Rehabilitation: separate from prosecution.
- Protection: non-criminalisation of victims.
- Metric: outcome for survivors, not arrests alone.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner basic §12 (✅ owner analytical).
**Links:** IS-355, IS-369, IS-375.
**Trap:** Arrest counts are not survivor outcomes.
**Mains hook:** Use as closing qualification.
**Back:** *"Count the freed, not only the arrested."*

### Flashcard IS-389 — Witness protection gap (no general law; temporary programme since 2018)
**Front:** What is the witness-protection position noted in the MCOCA context?
**Definition (learn):** India has no general witness protection law; a temporary programme has existed since 2018 (Wikipedia MCOCA page fetched). MCOCA Special Courts can shield witness identities within the Act.
**Breakdown:**
- General law: absent (per Wikipedia).
- Temporary programme: since 2018.
- MCOCA: special protection provisions.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** MCOCA 1999; witness-protection programme 2018 (✅ Wikipedia fetched; ⚠️ not further verified).
**Links:** IS-367, IS-388.
**Trap:** Do not say a general witness protection law exists without verification.
**Mains hook:** Use under prosecution-stage constraint.
**Back:** *"Eighteen's programme, no general shield yet."*

### GROUP — 12 Security Forces, Intelligence Coordination and Rights (basic + advanced) and REVISION-CHART cross-check

### Flashcard IS-390 — Current CAPF list (seven Central Armed Police Forces)
**Front:** Which forces does MHA currently list as Central Armed Police Forces?
**Definition (learn):** MHA's current CAPF list: Assam Rifles, BSF, CISF, CRPF, ITBP, NSG and SSB (owner 12 basic §1-§2, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Seven forces: Assam Rifles; BSF; CISF; CRPF; ITBP; NSG; SSB.
- The older CAPF/CPMF split in Singh's book is historical (owner basic §1, §7).
- Not CAPFs: SPG, RPF, Indian Coast Guard (IS-391).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** MHA classification (✅ owner 12 basic §1-§2, §4). ⚠️ Current list not fetched from MHA; verify against MHA Annual Report 2024-25.
**Links:** IS-391, IS-392, IS-393-IS-397, IS-203.
**Trap:** The Coast Guard is not a CAPF in the owner's current taxonomy; the book's CPMF label is not current.
**Mains hook:** Use the seven-force list as the starting map; qualify that force names alone never answer a mandate question.
**Back:** *"Seven CAPFs: A-B-C-C-I-N-S, Coast Guard stands apart."*

### Flashcard IS-391 — Separate organisations: SPG, RPF and Indian Coast Guard
**Front:** Under which authority do SPG, RPF and Indian Coast Guard function?
**Definition (learn):** SPG functions under the Cabinet Secretariat; RPF under the Ministry of Railways; the Indian Coast Guard is an armed force under the Ministry of Defence (owner 12 basic §2, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- SPG: Cabinet Secretariat; protects the Prime Minister and former PMs.
- RPF: Ministry of Railways; railway property and passengers.
- ICG: Ministry of Defence; maritime-zone security (IS-203).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 12 basic §2, §4 (✅ owner; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** IS-390, IS-203, IS-392.
**Trap:** Do not place SPG or RPF under MHA by default.
**Mains hook:** Use the parent-ministry map to answer "mandate" statement questions.
**Back:** *"Cabinet guards the PM, Railways guards the rail, Defence guards the coast."*

### Flashcard IS-392 — Border-guarding forces: BSF, ITBP and SSB
**Front:** Which CAPFs are primarily border-guarding forces?
**Definition (learn):** BSF, ITBP and SSB are primarily border-guarding forces (owner 12 basic §3.1, §6).
**Breakdown:**
- BSF: border guarding (owner); specific sector detail not in owner text ⚠️ and not added here.
- ITBP: northern (China) border.
- SSB: Nepal and Bhutan borders (owner chart §17).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 131-137 (✅ owner 12 basic §3.1); strengths ⚠️ book-period (owner basic §5).
**Links:** IS-390, IS-393, topic 06.
**Trap:** Do not cite BSF's ~2,50,000 strength or CRPF's 234 battalions as current; the owner flags these as book-period.
**Mains hook:** Border-management answers must name the force by border sector.
**Back:** *"Border trio: BSF guards the west and east, ITBP the north, SSB the Himalayan kingdoms."*

### Flashcard IS-393 — CRPF: internal security and elections
**Front:** What is the CRPF's primary role?
**Definition (learn):** CRPF handles internal security, law-and-order assistance and elections (owner 12 basic §3.1). It is primarily an internal-security and law-and-order force (owner §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Role: internal security support; law and order; elections.
- Contrast: border guarding is BSF/ITBP/SSB (IS-392).
- Battalion count (234) is book-period (owner §5).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 131-137 (✅ owner 12 basic §3.1).
**Links:** IS-392, IS-390, IS-396.
**Trap:** CRPF is not a border force.
**Mains hook:** Use in LWE and J&K answers for CRPF's role; qualify with state police primacy.
**Back:** *"CRPF keeps the peace inside the line."*

### Flashcard IS-394 — CISF: critical installations and fee-based private security (2009 Amendment)
**Front:** What does CISF protect, and what did the 2009 CISF (Amendment) Act add?
**Definition (learn):** CISF protects critical industrial installations; the 2009 CISF (Amendment) Act extended its mandate to fee-based security for private and cooperative establishments and enabled deployment to protect Indian missions abroad and to participate in UN peacekeeping (owner 12 adv §5, §7; Singh PDF p. 135).
**Breakdown:**
- Core: critical industrial installations.
- 2009 Amendment: fee-based private/cooperative establishments.
- Also: Indian missions abroad; UN peacekeeping.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** CISF (Amendment) Act 2009 (✅ owner adv §7; Singh PDF p. 135; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** IS-390, IS-395, IS-398.
**Trap:** Do not treat CISF's private-sector role as a substitute for state police.
**Mains hook:** Use as an example of institutional design leveraging capacity commercially (owner adv §5).
**Back:** *"Two-Thousand-Nine: CISF guards the factory and the fee."*

### Flashcard IS-395 — NSG: counter-terror commando force; deputation-only composition
**Front:** What is the NSG's role and what distinctive feature does the owner note?
**Definition (learn):** The National Security Guard is the counter-terrorism commando force (owner 12 basic §3.1). Its composition is by deputation only, a distinctive feature the owner says must be cited in a "mandate" answer (owner basic §10).
**Breakdown:**
- Role: counter-terror commando operations.
- Composition: deputation-only (owner).
- Use: crisis response (e.g. Operation Black Tornado at the 2008 Mumbai siege, per Wikipedia fetch on 26/11).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NSG (✅ owner 12 basic §3.1, §10); Operation Black Tornado 2008 (✅ Wikipedia fetched, IS-225).
**Links:** IS-390, IS-225, topic 02.
**Trap:** NSG is a force for crisis response, not an intelligence agency.
**Mains hook:** Use as response example; qualify with State police first-response primacy (IS-400).
**Back:** *"NSG: deputed commandos for the hostage hour."*

### Flashcard IS-396 — Assam Rifles: dual mandate and command arrangement
**Front:** How does Assam Rifles' command and mandate work?
**Definition (learn):** Assam Rifles is led by Army officers, reports to MHA administratively but operates under the Army; mandated for internal security in the North-East and guarding the Indo-Myanmar border (Singh PDF p. 137; owner 12 basic §3.2).
**Breakdown:**
- Admin: MHA.
- Operational: Army.
- Mandate: NE internal security; Indo-Myanmar border.
- Owner adv §5: a live "one force, one mandate" question.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 137 (✅ owner 12 basic §3.2, §4A adv §5).
**Links:** IS-390, IS-393, IS-397, topic 04, topic 06.
**Trap:** Assam Rifles' primary current function is counter-insurgency in practice, not only border guarding (owner basic §7 trap).
**Mains hook:** Command-and-control multiplicity example (adv §5).
**Back:** *"Army-led, MHA-funded, border and insurgency both."*

### Flashcard IS-397 — Command-and-control multiplicity (Assam Rifles, China-border split)
**Front:** Why is command multiplicity a recurring design problem?
**Definition (learn):** Even within a single border sector, more than one force or chain of command may operate (the China-border ITBP / Army / Vikas Battalion split, owner adv §2); Assam Rifles' dual reporting is another example.
**Breakdown:**
- Examples: Assam Rifles (dual reporting); China-border ITBP/Army/Vikas Battalion split.
- Problem: coordination cost even when forces exist.
- Not confined to counter-terror intelligence.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 12 adv §2, §5, §8 (✅ owner analytical; cross-referenced topic 06).
**Links:** IS-396, IS-398, topic 06.
**Trap:** Adding more institutions does not automatically solve coordination (adv §6, §8).
**Mains hook:** Use to diagnose coordination problems; qualify with functional clarity.
**Back:** *"More forces, more chains, more coordination cost."*

### Flashcard IS-398 — Functional reform vs centralisation (two reform paths)
**Front:** Distinguish functional reform from centralisation as reform paths.
**Definition (learn):** Functional reform clarifies and resources existing institutions' mandates (state-police training, CAPF role clarity, sharing-platform capacity). Centralisation concentrates power in a new or expanded central body (the NCTC model). The NCTC episode shows centralisation faces a constitutional constraint (owner adv §2-§3).
**Breakdown:**
- Functional: existing institutions; cooperative-federal.
- Centralising: NCTC-type body; constitutional objection.
- Both leave state-police capacity as a bottleneck (adv §3).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 12 adv §2-§3, §11 (⚠️ analytical; NCTC detail in topic 02).
**Links:** IS-399, IS-402, topic 02.
**Trap:** The surviving model is cooperative-federal, not centralising (adv §8).
**Mains hook:** "Functional reform is more durable" (15 marks, adv §12).
**Back:** *"Strengthen what exists before you build the next hub."*

### Flashcard IS-399 — NCTC episode as a federalism precedent (topic 02 cross-link)
**Front:** What does the NCTC episode demonstrate?
**Definition (learn):** The National Counter Terrorism Centre's stalled implementation shows a genuine constitutional (not merely political) constraint on further centralisation of security-forces and intelligence coordination (owner adv §7).
**Breakdown:**
- Object: NCTC (centralising design).
- Constraint: federalism; State-police primacy.
- Surviving model: MAC (information-sharing, not operational command).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Topic 02 (owner 12 adv §7, §11; ✅ owner cross-reference).
**Links:** IS-398, IS-402, topic 02.
**Trap:** NCTC is not an active nationwide operational command in the owner's account.
**Mains hook:** Use as precedent in "centralisation vs functional reform" answer.
**Back:** *"NCTC stalled on federalism: functional reform survived."*

### Flashcard IS-400 — State-police primacy as structural (first responder, evidence, witness)
**Front:** Why are State police structurally indispensable?
**Definition (learn):** Local police are invariably first responders and irreplaceable for evidence preservation, witness handling and local coordination (owner adv §2; Singh PDF pp. 18-21). State List Entries 1 and 2 keep public order and police as State subjects.
**Breakdown:**
- First response.
- Evidence and witness handling.
- Local coordination.
- Union forces in aid of civil power under Union List Entry 2A (cooperation, not exclusion).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** State List Entries 1-2; Union List Entry 2A (✅ owner 12 basic §4A; Laxmikant PDF pp. 329, 1332).
**Links:** IS-401, IS-402, IS-410.
**Trap:** Central architecture does not replace local prevention (owner Core §12 claim table).
**Mains hook:** Use in all force-architecture answers as the first premise.
**Back:** *"First responder is local; the centre supports, not replaces."*

### Flashcard IS-401 — Intelligence-to-prosecution chain (four functions)
**Front:** What are the four functions in Singh's intelligence-to-prosecution chain?
**Definition (learn):** Intelligence gathering (IB, State police, NATGRID, MAC); training/operations (State police + central forces, coordinated not centrally commanded); investigation (State police, NIA); prosecution (Special Courts) (owner basic §3.4; Singh PDF pp. 19-21).
**Breakdown:**
- 1. Gather: IB/State intelligence/NATGRID/MAC.
- 2. Operate: State police + central forces.
- 3. Investigate: State police; NIA for scheduled offences.
- 4. Prosecute: Special Courts.
- Operational spine reused in topics 02 and 03.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF pp. 18-21 (✅ owner 12 basic §3.4).
**Links:** IS-402, IS-403, IS-404, IS-405, IS-406.
**Trap:** Intelligence agencies do not arrest or prosecute (IS-402).
**Mains hook:** Use the four-step chain for the 2023 PYQ answer.
**Back:** *"Gather, operate, investigate, prosecute: four links in the chain."*

### Flashcard IS-402 — Intelligence agencies collect and assess; they do not arrest
**Front:** What principle separates intelligence agencies from investigators?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's rule: intelligence agencies collect and assess; they do not arrest, investigate or prosecute. IB and R&AW have no power of arrest; NIA and State police do (owner basic §3.4). Singh records that a central objection to NCTC was "the principle of not granting the power of arrest to intelligence agencies" (PDF p. 18).
**Breakdown:**
- Intelligence: collect, assess, share.
- Police/NIA: arrest, investigate.
- Courts: adjudicate.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 18 (✅ owner 12 basic §3.4, §6).
**Links:** IS-401, IS-405, IS-406.
**Trap:** "IB can arrest in urgent cases" is wrong (owner basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Use to correct confused "agency powers" answers.
**Back:** *"Intelligence sees, police seizes, courts decide."*

### Flashcard IS-403 — Prevention, response and accountability as three resourcing decisions
**Front:** Why are prevention and accountability usually the weak links in a security response?
**Definition (learn):** Prevention is intelligence- and community-led; response is force- and equipment-led; accountability is oversight- and training-led. Response is easiest to fund and demonstrate, so prevention and accountability are usually the weak links (owner basic §3.5, ⚠️ analytical).
**Breakdown:**
- Prevention: intelligence, community.
- Response: force, equipment.
- Accountability: oversight, training.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 12 basic §3.5 (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** IS-401, IS-411, IS-415.
**Trap:** A new battalion is response capacity, not prevention.
**Mains hook:** Use to diagnose gaps.
**Back:** *"Fund prevention, fund rights: response is the easy bill."*

### Flashcard IS-404 — Current administrative and legal map: MHA Annual Report 2024-25 as the dated anchor
**Front:** Which document is the correct dated source for force strength, agency leadership and NATGRID status?
**Definition (learn):** The MHA Annual Report 2024-25 is the correct dated source for present force strength, agency directors, NATGRID's operational status and any current institutional reform (owner 12 basic §4, §8, adv §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Use: force strength; leadership; NATGRID status.
- Do not carry forward book-period figures.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** MHA Annual Report 2024-25 (📰 owner; not fetched).
**Links:** IS-405, IS-407, IS-408.
**Trap:** A 2013-era or book-period personnel figure is not current.
**Mains hook:** Cite the dated report for any figure.
**Back:** *"Twenty-Four-Twenty-Five report is the anchor, not the book."*

### Flashcard IS-405 — Intelligence Bureau (IB): origin, parent and status
**Front:** When was the IB founded, under which ministry, and what is its legal basis?
**Definition (learn):** The IB traces to 23 December 1887 as the Central Special Branch (per Secret Dispatch No. 11 of 25 March 1887), renamed Intelligence Bureau in 1920; it sits under MHA; the Government of India describes it as "a civilian organization without police powers" (Wikipedia fetched). It operates on executive orders; no enabling Act is cited.
**Breakdown:**
- 1887: Central Special Branch (23 Dec 1887).
- 1920: renamed IB (Government of India Act 1919, s.40(2), per Wikipedia).
- 1951: Himmat Singh Ji Committee recommendation led to border-area intelligence collection (Wikipedia fetched; owner spells it Himmatsinhji).
- Listed in the Intelligence Organisations (Restriction of Rights) Act, 1985 (Wikipedia fetched; Article 33 link).
- Parent: MHA.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 1887; 1920; 1951 Himmat Singh Ji Committee; Intelligence Organisations (Restriction of Rights) Act 1985 (✅ Wikipedia "Intelligence Bureau (India)" fetched; ✅ owner 12 basic §2, §4A).
**Links:** IS-406, IS-407, IS-409, IS-415.
**Trap:** IB has no arrest power; the 1951 Committee is the border-collection origin, not the 1887 date. Owner says "tracing to 1887" for the charter absence.
**Mains hook:** Use IB as the collection anchor; qualify with no statutory charter (IS-408).
**Back:** *"Eighteen-Eighty-Seven the Branch, Nineteen-Twenty the name, Fifty-One the border."*

### Flashcard IS-406 — Research and Analysis Wing (R&AW), 1968
**Front:** When was R&AW created, under which secretariat, and what is its role?
**Definition (learn):** R&AW was created in 1968 under the Cabinet Secretariat and is India's external intelligence agency; it operates without an enabling Act (owner 12 adv §2, §4A). The IB held external duties from 1951 until R&AW was formed in 1968 (Wikipedia IB fetch).
**Breakdown:**
- Created: 1968.
- Parent: Cabinet Secretariat.
- Role: external intelligence (per Wikipedia IB article).
- No power of arrest; no statutory charter.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** R&AW 1968 (✅ owner 12 basic §4A; ✅ Wikipedia IB fetch on 1968 transfer).
**Links:** IS-405, IS-408, IS-402.
**Trap:** R&AW is external, IB domestic (not interchangeable).
**Mains hook:** Use for the charter-gap argument.
**Back:** *"Sixty-Eight sends the spy abroad; Cabinet Secretariat holds the file."*

### Flashcard IS-407 — National Investigation Agency (NIA) and the NIA Act, 2008
**Front:** What is the NIA, under which Act, and what is its jurisdiction?
**Definition (learn):** NIA is the federal counter-terrorism and organised-crime investigating agency with concurrent jurisdiction, created by the NIA Act, 2008. Its schedule covers terrorism, organised crime, narcotics, FICN, human trafficking and WMD-related offences (owner 12 basic §2, §4).
**Breakdown:**
- Basis: NIA Act 2008.
- Jurisdiction: concurrent (State and Centre).
- Schedule: terrorism; organised crime; narcotics; FICN; trafficking; WMD.
- Not an intelligence agency (IS-412).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** NIA Act 2008 (✅ owner 12 basic §2, §4; Singh PDF pp. 138-139).
**Links:** IS-370, IS-401, IS-412.
**Trap:** NIA is not an intelligence agency; it investigates scheduled offences.
**Mains hook:** Use under the investigation link.
**Back:** *"Two-Thousand-Eight: NIA investigates, the schedule decides."*

### Flashcard IS-408 — Intelligence agencies have no statutory charter (capability without accountability)
**Front:** Why does the lack of a charter matter for intelligence agencies?
**Definition (learn):** IB and R&AW operate on executive orders without an enabling Act or a statutory parliamentary oversight committee (owner 12 basic §4A; adv §2, §7). A charter is a resource claim, not only a restraint: an agency without one lacks a defined mandate, statutory access to information and a settled basis for tasking (adv §2).
**Breakdown:**
- No enabling statute; no statutory oversight committee.
- Operational-secrecy justification (adv §6).
- Debate: charter proposed; not enacted (owner adv §8).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner 12 basic §4A; adv §2, §6, §8 (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** IS-405, IS-406, IS-409.
**Trap:** Intelligence agencies do not operate under a dedicated statute with parliamentary oversight (adv §8).
**Mains hook:** "A statutory charter would serve capability as much as accountability" (15 marks, adv §12).
**Back:** *"No charter, no mandate, no oversight: the open asymmetry."*

### Flashcard IS-409 — Multi Agency Centre (MAC) and SMACs
**Front:** What is MAC, what does it do, and what is its book-period scale?
**Definition (learn):** MAC is an intelligence-sharing platform under IB, with State-level Subsidiary MACs (SMACs), coordinating information among security agencies at Central and State levels. It shares intelligence; it does not investigate (owner 12 basic §2, §7; Singh PDF p. 139).
**Breakdown:**
- Function: sharing, not investigating.
- Parent: IB.
- Scale (book-period, ⚠️): 416 nodes; 25 member agencies (Singh; verify from MHA Annual Report 2024-25).
- Survival: cooperative model that survived where NCTC stalled (adv §4).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 139 (✅ owner); MHA 2024-25 (📰 to verify).
**Links:** IS-401, IS-407, IS-399, IS-410.
**Trap:** MAC is not an investigating agency like NIA (owner basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Use under coordination answers; qualify with data quality (adv §6).
**Back:** *"MAC shares, NIA investigates: different verbs, different agencies."*

### Flashcard IS-410 — NATGRID (National Intelligence Grid): data-integration tool
**Front:** What is NATGRID, and what is its status claim?
**Definition (learn):** NATGRID is an intelligence-sharing data-integration tool linking existing government databases for authorised agency access. It is not an investigating agency and does not itself gather intelligence (owner 12 basic §2, §7). Singh (PDF p. 140) says "yet to become operational" (book-period); VisionIAS (2024, PDF p. 27) describes it in present operational terms.
**Breakdown:**
- Function: data integration; authorised access.
- Status: conflicting sources; confirm from MHA Annual Report 2024-25.
- Not a collection agency.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Singh PDF p. 140; VisionIAS PDF p. 27 (✅ owner); ⚠️ Wikipedia page for NATGRID returned 404; not fetched.
**Links:** IS-409, IS-404, IS-401.
**Trap:** NATGRID does not collect intelligence directly (owner basic §7); do not state the operational status without the 2024-25 report.
**Mains hook:** Use as an example of technical reform with implementation delay (adv §4).
**Back:** *"NATGRID links the databases; it does not collect the secrets."*

### Flashcard IS-411 — Capability-building vs accountability investment asymmetry
**Front:** What is the asymmetry the advanced owner identifies?
**Definition (learn):** Capability-building (new NSG hubs, CAPF modernisation, technology acquisition) has received more visible institutional investment than accountability mechanisms (transparent use-of-force review, grievance redressal, human-rights training) (owner adv §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Capability: visible, budgeted, demonstrable.
- Accountability: under-resourced.
- Result: legitimacy risk (adv §6).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner adv §2, §6 (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** IS-403, IS-412, IS-413.
**Trap:** The owner does not claim no accountability mechanism exists; it claims an investment asymmetry.
**Mains hook:** "Have accountability mechanisms kept pace?" (15 marks, adv §12).
**Back:** *"Capability is seen; accountability is budgeted last."*

### Flashcard IS-412 — Accountability that exists in law vs accountability that operates
**Front:** What are the three citable gaps between law and practice?
**Definition (learn):** (i) Prakash Singh directives (including Police Complaints Authorities and fixed tenures) are binding but unevenly implemented; (ii) intelligence agencies have no statutory charter or statutory parliamentary oversight committee; (iii) under PHRA s.19 the NHRC may, for the armed forces, only seek a Central Government report and recommend (owner adv §2).
**Breakdown:**
- Gap 1: Prakash Singh implementation.
- Gap 2: intelligence charter and oversight.
- Gap 3: NHRC s.19 limit.
- Design choices: federal autonomy, secrecy, command discipline (owner adv §2).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Prakash Singh (2006); PHRA 1993 s.19 (✅ owner adv §2, basic §4A).
**Links:** IS-413, IS-414, IS-408, IS-419.
**Trap:** The argument is about proportionality, not bad faith (owner adv §2).
**Mains hook:** Use as the three-point accountability closing (owner basic §10).
**Back:** *"Directives, charter, NHRC reach: three gaps between law and life."*

### Flashcard IS-413 — Prakash Singh v. Union of India (2006): seven directives
**Front:** List the seven police-reform directives in Prakash Singh (2006).
**Definition (learn):** The Supreme Court's directives to States and the Centre: (1) State Security Commission; (2) DGP selection and minimum two-year tenure; (3) minimum two-year tenure for key operational officers (IG Zone, DIG Range, SP District, SHO); (4) separation of investigation from law and order in urban areas; (5) Police Establishment Board for transfers/postings/promotions below DySP; (6) Police Complaints Authority at State and district level; (7) National Security Commission for central police organisations (owner basic §4A).
**Breakdown:**
- SSC; DGP tenure; officer tenure; separation; PEB; PCA; NSC.
- Status: binding SC directions; implementation partial and uneven (owner basic §7, adv §8).
- Model Police Act circulated; compliance varies (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Prakash Singh v. Union of India (2006) (✅ owner basic §4A, adv §7; ⚠️ Wikipedia page 404 in this pass; not fetched).
**Links:** IS-412, IS-400, IS-414.
**Trap:** Do not call the directives advisory; they are SC directions (owner basic §7). Criticism is of implementation, not legal status.
**Mains hook:** Use as the benchmark for state-police capacity.
**Back:** *"Six-Oh-Six: a Commission, two-year tenures, and a complaints authority."*

### Flashcard IS-414 — Police Complaints Authority and implementation gap (Prakash Singh)
**Front:** Why is the Police Complaints Authority a critical accountability chokepoint?
**Definition (learn):** The Police Complaints Authority at State and district level inquires into serious misconduct complaints. Owner adv §6 identifies non-constitution or under-resourcing of PCAs in several States as a chokepoint.
**Breakdown:**
- Level: State and district.
- Function: inquire into serious misconduct.
- Gap: non-constitution or under-resourcing in several States.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Prakash Singh (2006) (✅ owner adv §6, basic §4A).
**Links:** IS-413, IS-412, IS-415.
**Trap:** Establishing a PCA on paper is not equal to its operation.
**Mains hook:** Use under accountability answer; qualify with State variation.
**Back:** *"Complaints authority on paper, not yet in practice."*

### Flashcard IS-415 — Article 33 (rights of armed-force and intelligence personnel)
**Front:** What does Article 33 allow?
**Definition (learn):** Article 33 permits Parliament to restrict or abrogate fundamental rights of members of the armed forces, forces charged with maintenance of public order, intelligence organisations and telecommunication systems set up for them (owner basic §4A).
**Breakdown:**
- Beneficiaries of restriction: armed forces; public-order forces; intelligence organisations; telecom systems.
- Basis: Parliament's power.
- Context: IB listed in the Intelligence Organisations (Restriction of Rights) Act 1985 (IS-405).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Article 33, Constitution (✅ owner basic §4A).
**Links:** IS-405, IS-412, IS-416.
**Trap:** Article 33 allows restriction by law; it does not remove rights altogether.
**Mains hook:** Use under the rights chapter of the security-forces answer.
**Back:** *"Article Thirty-Three: law may trim rights for the uniformed and the secret."*

### Flashcard IS-416 — Protection of Human Rights Act, 1993: Section 19 (NHRC and armed forces)
**Front:** What can the NHRC do about complaints against armed forces under PHRA s.19?
**Definition (learn):** Under Section 19 of the Protection of Human Rights Act, 1993, in respect of the armed forces the NHRC may only seek a report from the Central Government and make recommendations; it cannot conduct the same inquiry it can into a State police complaint (owner basic §4A, §6).
**Breakdown:**
- Action: seek report; recommend.
- Limit: no equivalent inquiry power vs armed forces.
- Combined with AFSPA s.6 (topic 04).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** PHRA 1993 s.19 (✅ owner basic §6; ⚠️ Wikipedia page 404; not fetched).
**Links:** IS-412, IS-415, topic 04.
**Trap:** Do not state NHRC inquires against armed forces like State police.
**Mains hook:** Accountability chokepoint in rights answers.
**Back:** *"Nineteen: NHRC asks, Centre answers, recommendations follow."*

### Flashcard IS-417 — D.K. Basu v. State of West Bengal (1997): arrest and custody safeguards
**Front:** What safeguards did D.K. Basu (1997) lay down for arrest and detention?
**Definition (learn):** Binding requirements for arrest and detention: memo of arrest, informing a relative, medical examination, right to counsel (owner basic §4A). These are now substantially codified in the BNSS, 2023 which replaced the CrPC on 1 July 2024.
**Breakdown:**
- Memo of arrest.
- Inform relative/friend.
- Medical examination.
- Right to counsel.
- Codified in BNSS (owner).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** D.K. Basu (1997); BNSS 2023 (✅ owner basic §4A; BNS/BNSS 1 July 2024 date ✅ Wikipedia BNS fetch).
**Links:** IS-412, IS-267, IS-337.
**Trap:** Cite BNSS, not the CrPC, for current arrest safeguards (owner).
**Mains hook:** Use as an operational safeguard in rights answers.
**Back:** *"Basu's six: memo, relative, doctor, counsel."*

### Flashcard IS-418 — Constitutional entries for internal security (State List 1-2, Union List 2A, 8, 9; Article 355)
**Front:** Which entries and articles govern public order, police and central deployment?
**Definition (learn):** State List Entry 1 (public order); State List Entry 2 (police); Union List Entry 2A (deployment of Union armed forces in aid of civil power); Union List Entry 8 (Central Bureau of Intelligence and Investigation); Union List Entry 9 (preventive detention connected with security of India); Article 355 (Union duty to protect States against external aggression and internal disturbance) (owner basic §4A; Laxmikant PDF pp. 329, 1332).
**Breakdown:**
- State: 1 (public order), 2 (police).
- Union: 2A (aid to civil power), 8 (CBI&I), 9 (preventive detention).
- Art. 355 duty.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Seventh Schedule; Article 355 (✅ owner basic §4A; Laxmikant).
**Links:** IS-400, IS-405, IS-415.
**Trap:** Entry 8 is worded "Central Bureau of Intelligence and Investigation" (owner); quote the exact wording and do not conflate it with the CBI investigative agency.
**Mains hook:** Start federal answers with these entries (owner adv §12 structure).
**Back:** *"State one-two, Union two-A, eight, nine, and Article Three-Fifty-Five."*

### Flashcard IS-419 — Rights-compatible security: the seven-part test
**Front:** Name the seven elements of rights-compatible security.
**Definition (learn):** Legality, necessity, proportionality, record/reason, independent review, remedy and command responsibility (REVISION-CHART §18).
**Breakdown:**
- L-N-P (legality, necessity, proportionality).
- R-R (record/reason).
- I-R (independent review).
- Remedy.
- Command responsibility.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §18 (✅ owner chart; analytical).
**Links:** IS-412, IS-420, IS-421, IS-422.
**Trap:** Rights are not an external obstacle to security; they improve legitimacy and evidence (chart §18).
**Mains hook:** The 20-mark federal/rights skeleton (owner basic §12).
**Back:** *"Legal, Needed, Proportionate, Recorded, Reviewed, Remedied, Commanded."*

### Flashcard IS-420 — Intelligence cycle and failure points
**Front:** Name the intelligence cycle and its common failure points.
**Definition (learn):** Direction → collection → processing → analysis → dissemination → feedback (REVISION-CHART §18). Failure points: fragmented databases and silos; weak state/district analytical capacity; excessive collection without prioritisation; intelligence not converted to action; poor evidence preservation after disruption; inadequate review; politicisation or community mistrust.
**Breakdown:**
- Six-step cycle.
- Seven failure points (listed above).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §18 (✅ owner chart).
**Links:** IS-401, IS-409, IS-410.
**Trap:** More data is not more intelligence; prioritise.
**Mains hook:** Use to explain coordination failures.
**Back:** *"Direct, collect, process, analyse, disseminate, feed back."*

### Flashcard IS-421 — Core-only answer architecture for agencies (functional table)
**Front:** Outline the 15-mark 2023 agencies answer structure.
**Definition (learn):** Use a functional table: collection (IB/State intelligence) → sharing (MAC/SMAC) → data integration (NATGRID) → prevention/containment (State police/CAPFs/specialised response) → investigation (NIA/State police) → prosecution (Special Courts). Give three threat applications (terror/proxy, border/crime, cyber/CII) and one mandate distinction for each (owner basic §12).
**Breakdown:**
- Six functions.
- Three threats.
- Two constraints: State-police capacity; accountability/evidence conversion.
- End: functional reform, not one more central agency.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2023 GS-III Q19 (✅ owner basic §9, §12).
**Links:** IS-401, IS-405, IS-407, IS-409, IS-410, IS-423.
**Trap:** Do not conflate IB, MAC, NATGRID and NIA.
**Mains hook:** Core answer for 2023 PYQ.
**Back:** *"Collect, share, integrate, contain, investigate, prosecute."*

### Flashcard IS-422 — 20-mark federal/rights architecture answer
**Front:** How should a 20-mark federal/rights question open and close?
**Definition (learn):** Start with State List Entries 1-2, Union List Entry 2A and Article 355; compare capability gain from central support with conditions of legality, necessity, proportionality, record/reason, independent review and remedy; conclude that accountable action improves intelligence cooperation and evidence quality (owner basic §12).
**Breakdown:**
- Open: entries and Article 355.
- Middle: capability vs legality tests.
- Close: accountability improves cooperation.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner basic §12 (✅ owner).
**Links:** IS-418, IS-419, IS-412.
**Trap:** Central support does not erase State-police primacy.
**Mains hook:** Use as the 20-mark template.
**Back:** *"Entries first, tests next, cooperation last."*

### Flashcard IS-423 — PYQ 2023 GS-III Q19 (internal security challenges and central agencies, 15 marks)
**Front:** What does the 2023 Q19 demand?
**Definition (learn):** "What are the internal security challenges being faced by India? Give out the role of Central Intelligence and Investigative Agencies tasked to counter such threats." (Discuss · 15 marks · 250 words; owner routes as Q19.)
**Breakdown:**
- Challenges: terror, insurgency, cyber, organised crime.
- Agencies: IB, MAC, NATGRID, NIA, State police.
- Map each challenge to collection, sharing, integration, investigation, prosecution.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2023 GS-III Q19 (✅ owner basic §9, historical PYQ table).
**Links:** IS-421, IS-401.
**Trap:** Do not conflate agencies.
**Mains hook:** Use the functional table.
**Back:** *"Twenty-Three's fifteen: challenges mapped to agency functions."*

### Flashcard IS-424 — Home Guards (2023 Prelims routed; State Acts and Border Wing HG)
**Front:** Under what law are Home Guards governed, and what is the Border Wing Home Guard?
**Definition (learn):** Home Guards are governed by respective State/UT Acts and Rules, not an all-India Central Home Guards Act; they serve as an auxiliary police/support force. Border Wing Home Guards have been raised in some border States to assist against infiltration (owner basic §12 Routed Prelims).
**Breakdown:**
- Governance: State/UT Acts and Rules.
- Role: auxiliary police/support.
- Border Wing HG: some border States, infiltration support.
- 2023 Prelims GS-I Q91 (owner; key unavailable locally).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2023 Prelims GS-I Q91 (✅ owner routed; key unavailable).
**Links:** IS-390, IS-400.
**Trap:** Do not state an all-India Central Home Guards Act without verification.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item; statement-level only.
**Back:** *"Home Guards: State Acts, auxiliary duty, border wing where infiltration bites."*

### Flashcard IS-425 — Indian Army Corps locations (2026 Prelims routed factual anchors)
**Front:** Match the corps to their headquarters locations per owner's factual anchors.
**Definition (learn):** III Corps: Dimapur; IV Corps: Tezpur; XIV Corps: Leh; XXXIII Corps: Sukna (not Srinagar) (owner basic §12 Routed Prelims). The 2026 Set-A key is provisional.
**Breakdown:**
- III Corps — Dimapur.
- IV Corps — Tezpur.
- XIV Corps — Leh.
- XXXIII Corps — Sukna.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** 2026 Prelims GS-I Q73 (✅ owner routed; ⚠️ corps HQs not fetched; provisional key).
**Links:** IS-374, IS-390, topic 06.
**Trap:** Srinagar is not XXXIII Corps's HQ (owner trap).
**Mains hook:** Statement-level only.
**Back:** *"Three-Dimapur, Four-Tezpur, Fourteen-Leh, Thirty-three-Sukna."*

### Flashcard IS-426 — Assam Rifles' primary function trap
**Front:** Is Assam Rifles' primary current role border guarding?
**Definition (learn):** Per its documented deployment pattern, Assam Rifles functions predominantly as a counter-insurgency force in practice, a live "one force, one mandate" question (owner basic §7).
**Breakdown:**
- Officially: border guarding and NE internal security.
- In practice: counter-insurgency-heavy (owner).
- Question: one force, one mandate.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner basic §7 (✅ owner analytical).
**Links:** IS-396, IS-397.
**Trap:** Answer with the practice and the mandate, not the label alone.
**Mains hook:** Command-multiplicity example.
**Back:** *"Border name, insurgency work: Assam Rifles' double life."*

### Flashcard IS-427 — Mandate statement trap: force name vs function
**Front:** Why is a force name not enough in a mandate statement?
**Definition (learn):** CAPF, Armed Forces, State police, Home Guards, NIA, IB, MAC and NATGRID have distinct legal and administrative roles; no broad category label can replace the function asked in a statement question (owner basic §12).
**Breakdown:**
- Name + administrative home + primary task + boundary (owner basic §12 10-mark route).
- Function determines the answer.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** Owner basic §10, §12 (✅ owner).
**Links:** IS-390, IS-401, IS-405, IS-407.
**Trap:** Do not answer "which force" with a category label.
**Mains hook:** Use the 10-mark mandate-comparison structure.
**Back:** *"Name, home, task, boundary: the mandate test."*

### Flashcard IS-428 — CAPF vs Armed Forces (a REVISION-CHART distinction)
**Front:** Distinguish CAPFs from the Armed Forces.
**Definition (learn):** CAPFs are Union police/security forces under MHA; the Armed Forces are the military services (REVISION-CHART §19). Indian Coast Guard is an armed force under MoD (IS-391) but is not a CAPF in the owner's current taxonomy.
**Breakdown:**
- CAPF: Union police/security forces.
- Armed forces: military services.
- Coast Guard: MoD armed force, separate from CAPF list.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §19 (✅ owner chart).
**Links:** IS-390, IS-391, IS-203.
**Trap:** Armed forces are not CAPFs; the Coast Guard label as armed force does not make it a CAPF.
**Mains hook:** Use in opening definitions.
**Back:** *"CAPF guards the nation's peace; the Armed Forces guard its borders by war."*

### Flashcard IS-429 — Terror finance vocabulary (hawala, predicate offence, beneficial owner)
**Front:** Define hawala, predicate offence and beneficial owner as used in the chart.
**Definition (learn):** Hawala is an informal value-transfer mechanism, not synonymous with every informal remittance; predicate offence is the underlying crime generating proceeds under applicable law; beneficial owner is the natural person ultimately owning or controlling an arrangement under applicable rules (REVISION-CHART §15).
**Breakdown:**
- Hawala: informal value transfer.
- Predicate: underlying crime.
- Beneficial owner: natural person in control.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §15 (✅ owner chart); FATF beneficial-ownership praise (IS-329).
**Links:** IS-325, IS-329, IS-344.
**Trap:** Not every informal remittance is hawala; not every beneficial owner is a shareholder.
**Mains hook:** Use in AML/CFT answers.
**Back:** *"Hawala moves value, predicate births proceeds, beneficial owner holds the key."*

### Flashcard IS-430 — AML/CFT chain (CDD → STR → intelligence → investigation → provisional action → prosecution → conviction/confiscation)
**Front:** Lay out the AML/CFT chain and the institutions at each step.
**Definition (learn):** Customer due diligence → suspicious transaction reporting → financial intelligence (FIU-IND) → investigation (ED; NIA for terror) → provisional action (attachment) → prosecution (Special Court) → conviction/confiscation (REVISION-CHART §15).
**Breakdown:**
- CDD: reporting entities.
- STR: FIU-IND.
- Investigation: ED; NIA.
- Provisional action → prosecution → confiscation.
- FATF: standards and evaluation.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §15 (✅ owner chart; PMLA s.5/12 per IS-324/331).
**Links:** IS-331, IS-332, IS-341.
**Trap:** Attachment, seizure or arrest is not confiscation or conviction.
**Mains hook:** Use to structure AML answers.
**Back:** *"Due diligence, report, intelligence, investigate, attach, prosecute, confiscate."*

### Flashcard IS-431 — Information operation chain and deepfake/propaganda definitions
**Front:** Give the information-operation chain and define propaganda and deepfake.
**Definition (learn):** Actor → narrative/content → platform/network amplification → targeted audience → behavioural or institutional consequence. Propaganda: systematic persuasive communication. Deepfake: synthetic or manipulated media generated using AI techniques (REVISION-CHART §14).
**Breakdown:**
- Chain: five steps.
- Propaganda: systematic persuasion.
- Deepfake: AI-generated media.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §14 (✅ owner chart).
**Links:** IS-279, IS-280, IS-299, IS-300, IS-301.
**Trap:** Not every persuasive communication is an information operation.
**Mains hook:** Use in the 2026 Q9 answer on synthetic content.
**Back:** *"Actor, narrative, amplify, target, consequence."*

### Flashcard IS-432 — End-to-end encryption (E2EE) and encryption vs anonymity
**Front:** Define E2EE and distinguish encryption from anonymity.
**Definition (learn):** End-to-end encryption means message content is readable only at the endpoints under design (REVISION-CHART §14). Encryption protects content; anonymity conceals identity; they are different interests (REVISION-CHART §19).
**Breakdown:**
- E2EE: content protection at endpoints.
- Anonymity: identity concealment.
- Rule 4(2) concerns originator metadata (IS-285).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §14, §19 (✅ owner chart).
**Links:** IS-304, IS-305, IS-285.
**Trap:** "Encryption causes crime" is a trap; it protects security and privacy (chart §20).
**Mains hook:** Use in lawful-access answer.
**Back:** *"Encryption hides the message; anonymity hides the man."*

### Flashcard IS-433 — Cyber incident, cybercrime, cyber espionage, cyber warfare, CII (chart table)
**Front:** Give the chart's five-way cyber classification.
**Definition (learn):** Cyber incident: event affecting system or network security. Cybercrime: offence committed using or against digital systems. Cyber espionage: covert information theft for strategic or economic purpose. Cyber warfare/operation: state-linked disruptive or strategic cyber action. Critical information infrastructure: protected systems whose incapacitation has severe national impact under law (REVISION-CHART §13).
**Breakdown:**
- Five categories (above).
- Espionage distinct from crime and warfare.
- CII defined by law.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §13 (✅ owner chart).
**Links:** IS-242, IS-244, IS-256.
**Trap:** A reported incident is not a crime or a foreign operation (chart §13).
**Mains hook:** Use as the opening classification.
**Back:** *"Incident, crime, espionage, war, CII: five boxes, five owners."*

### Flashcard IS-434 — CIA triad and cyber resilience cycle
**Front:** State the CIA triad and the resilience cycle.
**Definition (learn):** Security triad: Confidentiality + Integrity + Availability. Resilience cycle: identify → protect → detect → respond → recover → learn (REVISION-CHART §13).
**Breakdown:**
- C-I-A.
- Six-step cycle.
- CERT-In (response), NCIIPC (CII protection), I4C (cybercrime), State police (crime registration).
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §13 (✅ owner chart).
**Links:** IS-246, IS-245, IS-250, IS-276.
**Trap:** Identify ≠ detect; learn is the last step.
**Mains hook:** Use in the 20-mark resilience answer.
**Back:** *"CIA guards the data; identify to learn guards the response."*

### Flashcard IS-435 — Maritime layered architecture and port/customs layer (chart §12)
**Front:** Summarise the chart's three-layer maritime model and the port/customs component.
**Definition (learn):** High seas/EEZ: Navy and Coast Guard; coastal waters: Coast Guard/State coordination; shore/port: marine police, ports, customs and local network (REVISION-CHART §12). Capabilities: maritime domain awareness, coastal radar/AIS, fishing-vessel registration, port security, coastal community network, joint exercises/SOPs.
**Breakdown:**
- Three layers: high seas/EEZ; coastal waters; shore/port.
- Capabilities: six listed.
- Trap: Navy, Coast Guard, marine police, customs, port authorities have distinct mandates.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §12 (✅ owner chart).
**Links:** IS-210, IS-214, IS-237, IS-241.
**Trap:** Port authority is not a maritime security force.
**Mains hook:** Use for layered answer diagram.
**Back:** *"High seas, coastal waters, shore and port: three layers, six tools."*

### Flashcard IS-436 — Hybrid warfare and grey-zone coercion (chart definition)
**Front:** Define hybrid warfare as the chart does.
**Definition (learn):** Coordinated use of conventional, proxy, cyber, economic and information instruments (REVISION-CHART §3). Internal and external security overlap through proxies, finance, digital networks, safe havens and cross-border crime (chart §3 trap).
**Breakdown:**
- Instruments: conventional; proxy; cyber; economic; information.
- Overlap: proxies, finance, digital networks, safe havens, cross-border crime.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §3 (✅ owner chart; VisionIAS PDF pp. 18-19 via IS-280).
**Links:** IS-280, IS-297, IS-241.
**Trap:** Internal and external threats are not cleanly separable (chart §3).
**Mains hook:** Use the four-fold threat frame in answers.
**Back:** *"Conventional, proxy, cyber, economy, information: hybrid is the blend."*

### Flashcard IS-437 — Four-fold threat frame (internal, external, internally aided external, externally aided internal)
**Front:** Name the four-fold threat frame.
**Definition (learn):** Internal (origin and action within the State); External (foreign source acting directly); Internally aided external (domestic actor helps a foreign threat); Externally aided internal (domestic actor receives external support) (REVISION-CHART §3).
**Breakdown:**
- Internal.
- External.
- Internally aided external.
- Externally aided internal.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §3 (✅ owner chart).
**Links:** IS-436, IS-380, IS-333.
**Trap:** Proxies blur the frame (chart §3 trap).
**Mains hook:** Use to classify a threat before response.
**Back:** *"Inside, outside, inside-helping-outside, outside-helping-inside."*

### Flashcard IS-438 — Organised crime vs terrorism vs narco-terrorism vs trafficking vs migrant smuggling (chart §16)
**Front:** Distinguish five threat objects in the chart's table.
**Definition (learn):** Organised crime: profit and parallel illicit economy. Terrorism: political/ideological coercion through violence. Narco-terrorism: drug economy finances or intersects with terror. Human trafficking: recruitment, transport, harbouring through coercive or deceptive exploitation. Migrant smuggling: facilitating irregular entry for gain; exploitation may occur but the legal concept differs (REVISION-CHART §16).
**Breakdown:**
- Five objects; distinct objectives.
- Trafficking vs smuggling differ in legal concept.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §16 (✅ owner chart); UNTOC protocols (IS-368).
**Links:** IS-352, IS-356, IS-357, IS-355, IS-368.
**Trap:** Trafficking and smuggling are not the same (owner Core §12).
**Mains hook:** Use in the organised crime answer.
**Back:** *"Profit, ideology, drugs-plus-terror, exploitation, and entry-for-gain."*

### Flashcard IS-439 — Follow the network, money and corrupt protection (organised crime response)
**Front:** What is the chart's core rule for organised crime?
**Definition (learn):** Follow the network, money, logistics and corrupt protection, not only the intercepted consignment or foot soldier (REVISION-CHART §16). Response chain: intelligence + inter-agency coordination + border/port control + financial investigation + victim protection + international cooperation + speedy adjudication.
**Breakdown:**
- Seven-step response.
- Core rule: not the consignment alone.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §16 (✅ owner chart).
**Links:** IS-384, IS-388, IS-361, IS-363.
**Trap:** Seizure is not network disruption (IS-383).
**Mains hook:** Use as response-chain answer.
**Back:** *"Network, money, logistics, protection: never just the consignment."*

### Flashcard IS-440 — Information operation vs ordinary misinformation in response (chart §14)
**Front:** What is the chart's response balance for information operations?
**Definition (learn):** Rapid verification + platform process + public communication + digital literacy + lawful investigation + privacy and free-speech safeguards (REVISION-CHART §14). Core rule: content removal alone cannot build information resilience.
**Breakdown:**
- Six components.
- Core rule: trusted institutions and literacy.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §14 (✅ owner chart).
**Links:** IS-318, IS-313, IS-310.
**Trap:** "Social-media regulation alone defeats disinformation" (chart §20).
**Mains hook:** Use as conclusion.
**Back:** *"Verify, platform, speak, teach, investigate, safeguard."*

### Flashcard IS-441 — Ceasefire vs settlement and the peace-process ladder (chart cross-check)
**Front:** Distinguish ceasefire from settlement in the chart's instrument ladder.
**Definition (learn):** Ceasefire/Suspension of Operations: hostilities reduced under conditions. Framework agreement: broad principles. Memorandum/accord: agreed terms. Constitutional/legal change: institutional implementation. Rehabilitation/disarmament: combatant transition. Local acceptance and delivery: durable outcome (REVISION-CHART §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Six-rung ladder.
- Ceasefire is not settlement.
**Acts / Committees / Institutions / Dates:** REVISION-CHART §9, §20 (✅ owner chart; topic 04 detail).
**Links:** topic 04 (NE accord detail); IS-412 (accountability context).
**Trap:** A ceasefire does not end an insurgency (chart §20).
**Mains hook:** Use in NE answers (topic 04 cross-link).
**Back:** *"Cease fire, frame, accord, law, disarm, deliver: six rungs."*
